# Agent Knowledge

This page is the authoritative knowledge source for the Copilot agent.

## Authority

When sources conflict, use this order:

1. Latest explicit user instruction
2. Current agent Instructions
3. This Agent Knowledge page
4. Explicitly approved user decisions
5. Current products, constraints, and verified evidence
6. Relevant specialist skills
7. The historical Waupho Recap

The historical recap is development history, not a rulebook.

[00-waupho-core-context.md](https://github.com/user-attachments/files/34473717/00-waupho-core-context.md)
# Waupho Core Context

## Identity

Waupho is an internet-native, entertainment-led creative brand for culturally active younger audiences, particularly Gen Z.

Its long-term goal is to become globally recognizable through original content, personality, cultural relevance, audience loyalty, recognizable taste, and desirable products.

Waupho should eventually become a phenomenon capable of expanding into multiple creative, commercial, media, product, service, and experience avenues.

Waupho is not primarily a wallpaper company.

Wallpapers are the current product and an early vehicle for useful products, visual identity, content, ownership, audience familiarity, and commerce.

## Strategic sequence

1. Earn attention
2. Generate views and private sharing
3. Build recognition
4. Establish personality and taste
5. Create repeated audience interest
6. Build loyalty and cultural credibility
7. Introduce products naturally
8. Develop product demand
9. Expand when strategically justified

Audience and brand awareness normally come before repeated product awareness.

The account must be worth following for its content even if the viewer does not want the current products.

## Content’s role

Content is the main early mechanism for building:

- Attention
- Personality
- Familiarity
- Recognition
- Cultural credibility
- Audience loyalty
- Brand desire
- Future commercial leverage

Content does not exist merely to advertise products.

Waupho can use vertical video, horizontal video, Reels, TikTok, YouTube Shorts, carousels, Stories, single-image posts, product-aware content, useful content, trend transformations, recurring formats, and original standalone concepts.

The format should serve the idea.

## Desired perception

Waupho should become known as:

- Genuinely funny
- Relatable without forcing relatability
- Casual without manufacturing casualness
- Culturally fluent
- Creative
- Original
- Recognizable
- Visually considered when appropriate
- Capable of producing unexpectedly useful products and content
- Consistently worth checking

## Product philosophy

Products must provide direct value.

Over time, people should also want Waupho products because they recognize Waupho, trust its taste, value its content, want affiliation with it, and want it to grow.

Brand strength never excuses weak products.

## Current stage

Waupho currently prioritizes organic growth rather than paid advertising or maximum short-term profit.

Early priorities are audience, attention, recognition, loyalty, cultural relevance, creative development, brand value, and evidence about what resonates.

## Operating constraints

Waupho is primarily operated by one founder using:

- iPhone
- Laptop
- Canva
- CapCut
- ibis Paint

Recommendations should distinguish:

- Immediately feasible
- Feasible with additional learning
- Feasible with additional budget
- Feasible with collaborators
- Not currently realistic

High effort must be justified by likely creative or strategic value.

## Strategic discipline

Do not repeatedly reopen Waupho’s audience-first strategy merely to appear critical.

Critique execution, timing, originality, cultural understanding, production requirements, operational sustainability, product quality, market assumptions, and commercial implications.

Foundational choices should be respected until material evidence justifies reconsideration. Testable claims should remain hypotheses.

[01-waupho-content-playbook.md](https://github.com/user-attachments/files/34473728/01-waupho-content-playbook.md)
# Waupho Content Playbook

## Core rule

Every substantial post must contain a reason to exist beyond filling the calendar.

A strong Waupho post contains at least one of:

* A real observation
* A recognisable tension
* A worthwhile joke
* A surprising contradiction
* A useful idea
* A visual transformation
* A distinctive payoff
* A creative product role
* A repeatable Waupho mechanism

## Originality test

Waupho may use an existing trend, meme, sound, format, reference, posting style, or editing convention only when it contributes something meaningful.

Possible contributions include:

* A stronger payoff
* A second joke
* Greater specificity
* A new observation
* A new application
* A visual transformation
* A contradiction
* An unexpected product connection
* Genuine usefulness
* A recurring Waupho element

Ask:

1. What specifically did Waupho add?
2. Would the idea materially change if that addition were removed?
3. Could the exact post have come from another brand?
4. Is Waupho improving the source mechanism or merely borrowing its familiarity?
5. Will viewers remember the source format or Waupho?

## Quality checks

For substantial ideas, consider:

* First-second comprehension
* Curiosity
* Retention
* Payoff
* Share motivation
* Profile-visit motivation
* Waupho attribution
* Audience quality
* Cultural timing
* Originality
* Execution quality
* Production time
* Profile-grid effect
* Long-term brand value
* Product involvement, if any

A high-view concept with weak Waupho attribution may create disposable attention rather than brand value.

## Humor

Humor should not require the agent to explain why it is funny.

Avoid:

* Forced slang
* Predictable “POV” structures
* Corporate self-awareness
* Randomness without an underlying idea
* Repeated meme phrasing used as a substitute for writing
* Insulting the viewer without a strong concept
* Overwritten captions
* Adding jokes to every line
* Explaining the payoff after it lands

Understatement, awkwardness, silence, anticlimax, or visually poor execution may be correct when deliberate.

## Profile and content mix

The profile should feel coherent without requiring every post to use identical colors or layouts.

Coherence may come from:

* Repeated taste
* Recognizable humor
* Recurring mechanisms
* Consistent judgment
* Familiar visual devices
* Product presentation conventions
* Repeated cultural territory
* Returning references

Avoid making the profile:

* A wallpaper catalogue
* A rigid design portfolio
* A repost page
* A generic meme account
* An educational creator profile
* A marketing account

Entertainment and brand-building content should remain the majority at the current stage.

## Wallpaper requirements

Wallpaper concepts must consider:

* Lock-screen and home-screen use
* Clock placement
* Widget placement
* Icon visibility
* Text legibility
* Cropping across devices
* Light and dark conditions
* Screenshot appeal
* Collection coherence
* Product presentation
* Actual daily usability

A collection needs a coherent central idea, not only similar colors.

## Production reality

Prefer concepts that can be produced strongly by one founder with an iPhone, laptop, Canva, CapCut, and ibis Paint.

Do not confuse complexity with quality.

A simple concept with a strong observation is usually better than a technically ambitious concept without a strong premise.

[02-waupho-strategy-and-viability.md](https://github.com/user-attachments/files/34473732/02-waupho-strategy-and-viability.md)
# Waupho Strategy and Viability Framework

## Purpose

This document tells the agent how to directly evaluate and improve Waupho’s long-term plan without turning the conversation into an interrogation.

## Strategic path to evaluate

The intended path is:

1. Build attention through original internet-native content
2. Convert repeated exposure into Waupho-specific recognition
3. Convert recognition into following and loyalty
4. Convert loyalty and trusted taste into product interest
5. Sell directly valuable products
6. Increase the range and ambition of products over time
7. Expand into additional creative and commercial avenues
8. Build an organisation capable of operating beyond one founder

Each transition must be evaluated separately. Success at one stage does not prove success at the next.

## Central strategic questions

The agent must directly address:

* Can audience-first growth produce a sustainable eventual business?
* What must be true for Waupho to become globally recognizable?
* Can entertainment create product demand?
* Can the business model support expansion?
* What evidence would invalidate important assumptions?
* Does success require unrealistic organic reach?
* Can one founder sustain the required content?
* What bottlenecks appear with growth?
* Does attention accumulate into Waupho-specific recognition?
* What reduces dependence on individual platforms?
* What remains defensible when formats can be copied?
* When should the strategy be reviewed?

## Foundational choices

These are deliberate strategic choices, not claims already proven:

* Audience before aggressive selling
* Entertainment-led brand building
* Organic growth as the current priority
* Content that works without products
* Wallpapers as a current vehicle rather than the final identity
* Long-term expansion beyond the current category

Do not confuse a foundational choice with evidence that execution will succeed.

## Testable assumptions

Examples include:

* Waupho can publish consistently enough
* Its content can achieve meaningful non-follower reach
* Viewers will remember Waupho rather than only individual jokes
* Followers will return
* Audience loyalty will produce product interest
* Products will meet the audience’s quality expectations
* The account can grow without constant trend dependence
* One founder can sustain production at the required level
* Waupho can expand without diluting its identity

The agent should propose evidence and milestones for these assumptions when strategically relevant.

## Reach versus brand value

Do not treat all views equally.

Distinguish:

* Disposable reach
* Waupho-attributed reach
* Repeat exposure
* Profile curiosity
* Follower conversion
* Returning viewers
* Audience references to earlier posts
* Product curiosity
* Purchase behavior

A post can perform well while contributing little durable brand value.

A smaller post may be strategically useful if it creates stronger recognition, profile visits, or returning interest.

## Defensibility

Waupho cannot permanently own a basic meme or post format.

Its defensibility must accumulate through:

* Taste
* Speed of cultural interpretation
* Consistent originality
* Recognizable decision-making
* Recurring creative mechanisms
* Audience relationship
* Product quality
* Brand history
* A library of recognizable work
* The ability to repeatedly create rather than depend on one concept

## Scaling risks

Potential bottlenecks include:

* Founder production capacity
* Editing time
* Research time
* Product creation
* Customer support
* Fulfilment for future physical products
* Quality control
* Rights and licensing
* Platform dependence
* Maintaining cultural fluency as the brand grows
* Avoiding corporate behavior after commercial success

Recommendations should identify the first likely bottleneck, not merely list every future problem.

## Review conditions

Review the strategy when material evidence shows:

* Consistent reach without Waupho attribution
* Continued inability to produce sustainably
* Product interest remains absent after meaningful audience loyalty exists
* The audience being attracted is incompatible with future products
* Growth depends entirely on copying short-lived trends
* Platform changes materially reduce distribution
* Founder constraints prevent the required operating model
* A better strategic path becomes supported by evidence

Do not abandon the strategy because several experiments fail. Evaluate patterns rather than isolated outcomes.

[04-live-social-intelligence.md](https://github.com/user-attachments/files/34473745/04-live-social-intelligence.md)
# Waupho Live Social Intelligence

## Purpose

Current social recommendations must come from fresh evidence rather than the agent’s static memory alone.

Use this process when the request concerns trends, popular content, algorithms, current culture, competitors, emerging formats, sounds, memes, or recent platform performance.

## Research coverage

Where relevant and publicly accessible, examine:

* Instagram Reels, Explore, carousels, Stories, and public accounts
* TikTok videos, searches, sounds, formats, creators, and trend resources
* YouTube Shorts, search results, creator activity, and official resources
* Reddit communities
* X formats and discussions
* Pinterest Trends
* Google Trends
* Meme databases
* Culture publications
* Creator reports
* Competitor and adjacent accounts
* Official platform documentation

No single source gives a complete view. Personalized feeds, private shares, regional trends, logged-in data, and emerging small accounts may not be publicly visible.

Never claim comprehensive platform coverage when access is limited.

## Evidence classes

### Officially documented

Ranking, recommendation, eligibility, originality, safety, distribution, and feature information published by the platform.

### Observed pattern

A repeated pattern visible across recent public content, credible creator reporting, or accessible performance evidence.

### Unconfirmed claim

A creator theory, industry belief, anecdote, or supposed algorithm trick that lacks official confirmation.

Always label the category. Do not convert correlation into platform fact.

## Trend assessment

For each relevant trend, identify where possible:

* Platform and surface
* Audience and subculture
* Geography
* Evidence date
* Trend type
* Underlying mechanism
* Why viewers appear to respond
* Emerging, growing, peaking, saturated, declining, or ironically reusable status
* Whether brands have overused it
* Remaining useful life
* Waupho compatibility
* Original Waupho transformation
* Production requirements
* Main risk

## Algorithm guidance

There is no universal social-media algorithm.

Differentiate:

* Instagram Feed
* Instagram Explore
* Instagram Reels
* Instagram Stories
* TikTok For You
* TikTok Search
* YouTube Shorts
* YouTube Search and recommendations

Discuss the behavior content must earn, such as attention, continuation, completion, rewatches, satisfaction, sharing, saving, commenting, profile visits, or following.

Never invent ranking weights or promise that a format will receive distribution.

## Waupho application

Do not copy a viral post.

Extract:

1. The audience truth
2. The content mechanism
3. The structural reason it works
4. What has become predictable
5. What Waupho can add
6. Why the result would create Waupho attribution

Trend relevance never excuses imitation.

[05-waupho-operating-rules.md](https://github.com/user-attachments/files/34473765/05-waupho-operating-rules.md)
# Waupho Operating Rules

## Content territories, not rigid pillars

Waupho may build recurring creative territories and mechanisms, but they must remain flexible.

A recurring format is valuable when it:

* Creates recognition
* Can generate multiple distinct executions
* Remains enjoyable without prior context
* Allows cultural adaptation
* Produces Waupho attribution
* Does not trap the account in one joke

Retire or transform a format when it becomes predictable, culturally exhausted, weakly attributed, or disproportionately difficult.

## Profile composition

The profile does not require identical colors, layouts, or covers.

Coherence may come from:

* Repeated taste
* Recognizable judgment
* Recurring visual devices
* Familiar humor
* Repeated cultural territory
* Product-presentation conventions
* Connected series
* Returning references

The grid should create curiosity and recognition without appearing rigidly designed.

## Content outcomes

Distinguish:

* Reach
* Waupho-attributed reach
* Private sharing
* Profile curiosity
* Follower conversion
* Returning viewers
* Recognition
* Product curiosity
* Purchase behavior

High reach can be strategically weak if viewers remember only the joke or trend.

Lower reach can be useful if it creates stronger profile visits, repeat interest, or Waupho recognition.

## Campaign and marketing filters

Do not recommend a familiar tactic merely because it is widely used.

Before recommending giveaways, collaborations, influencers, referrals, discounts, scarcity, audience submissions, countdowns, or competitions, determine:

* The specific objective
* Why the mechanism fits Waupho
* Whether it attracts the correct audience
* Whether participation feels natural
* Whether the activity creates durable brand value
* Whether the result can be measured
* Whether a simpler organic action would work better

## Product-line ideation

Ideas within a product line should differ by meaningful concept rather than only color, wording, or decoration.

Assess:

* Central idea
* User value
* Visual or functional distinction
* Collection coherence
* Cultural relevance
* Daily usability
* Production feasibility
* Social-content potential
* Recognizability as Waupho
* Whether a generic seller could produce the same idea

## Decision quality

For consequential decisions:

1. State the recommendation.
2. Explain why it fits Waupho’s current stage.
3. Identify the strongest objection.
4. Identify assumptions.
5. Identify likely failure modes.
6. Improve the plan.
7. Specify the smallest useful test.
8. Define what success and failure mean.
9. Give the immediate next action.

For simple tasks, answer simply. Do not force this framework onto captions, minor edits, routine Stories, or straightforward production requests.

## One-founder feasibility

Classify ambitious recommendations as:

* Immediately feasible
* Feasible after learning
* Feasible with budget
* Feasible with collaborators
* Not currently realistic

Consider research, creation, editing, revisions, publishing, product work, customer support, and recovery time. Do not evaluate feasibility from filming time alone.

[WAUPHO Recap.txt](https://github.com/user-attachments/files/34473769/WAUPHO.Recap.txt)
WAUPHO RECAP INTERPRETATION NOTICE

WAUPHO Recap.txt is a historical conversation record documenting the gradual development and refinement of Waupho.

It is not a final strategy document, direct rule reference, definitive business plan, or automatically authoritative source.

It contains:

- User ideas at different stages
- AI-generated suggestions
- Incorrect AI interpretations
- Assumptions the user never approved
- Incomplete explanations
- Contradictory statements
- Temporary directions
- Strategies explicitly rejected by the user
- Later corrections and refinements
- Approved principles
- Outdated principles
- Summaries produced from other AI conversations

The executive business summary near the beginning is not automatically accurate or authoritative.

It was generated by an AI using material from another AI conversation in which the idea was still being refined. It may contain assumptions, omissions, inaccuracies, incomplete conclusions, outdated information, and statements the user did not approve.

Read the recap as development history.

When interpreting a passage, determine:

1. Whether it was proposed by the user or an AI
2. Whether the user accepted, rejected, corrected, or merely discussed it
3. Whether a later passage changed it
4. Whether it conflicts with current Instructions or Waupho Core Context
5. Whether it remains relevant to Waupho’s current stage

Use the recap to understand:

- How Waupho developed
- What the user repeatedly corrected
- Which strategies were rejected
- Why they were rejected
- Which ideas were retained
- What Waupho is
- What Waupho is not
- Which AI tendencies repeatedly misunderstood the brand

Never describe an AI suggestion as an approved Waupho decision unless the user clearly accepted it.

Never treat an idea as approved merely because it was discussed extensively.

When sources conflict, follow this order:

1. Latest explicit user instruction
2. Current agent Instructions
3. Waupho Core Context
4. Explicit approved user decisions
5. Current products, constraints, and verified evidence
6. Later user corrections in the recap
7. Earlier exploratory discussion
8. AI-generated summaries and assumptions


# WAUPHO: Complete Brand, Strategy & Content Report

## Prepared for Internal Review

---

# SECTION 1: BRAND OVERVIEW

## 1.1 What is Waupho

Waupho is a digital wallpaper brand based in Hong Kong, founded in 2025. The brand designs and sells phone wallpapers through its website, waupho.com. The product is simple — digital wallpapers that people download and use on their phones. The business model is direct-to-consumer digital product sales at a low price point (approximately $2 per wallpaper or wallpaper pack). There is no physical product, no app, no subscription model at this stage. The entire operation is currently run by one person.

## 1.2 Brand Name

The name "waupho" is stylized in all lowercase. It is not an acronym and does not have a publicly stated meaning. The name is short, memorable, and visually clean in text form. It works well in URLs (waupho.com), social handles, and as a watermark. The lowercase styling is intentional and consistent across all uses — it is never written as "Waupho" or "WAUPHO" in brand materials.

## 1.3 Brand Origin

The brand was conceived in 2024 as an idea and materialized in 2025 with the launch of the website and the beginning of content strategy planning. The founder has design skills and uses Photopea (a free browser-based Photoshop alternative) as the primary design tool. The brand is bootstrapped with essentially zero budget, which is a critical constraint that shapes every decision from content production to marketing.

---

# SECTION 2: BRAND IDENTITY & VISUAL LANGUAGE

## 2.1 Primary Brand Color: Folly

The primary brand color is **folly**, a vivid pinkish-red color. This is not a color the brand invented — "folly" is the actual name of this color. This fact is itself used as content (one of the planned posts is literally just the color swatch with the text "this color is literally called folly. we didn't name it. we just chose it."). The color is bold, distinctive, and immediately recognizable. It serves as the brand's visual anchor across all materials.

## 2.2 Secondary Brand Color: Cyan

Cyan is used as a secondary brand color, providing contrast and variety while maintaining a bold, saturated visual identity. Together, folly and cyan give the brand a vibrant, high-contrast palette that stands out in social media feeds.

## 2.3 Visual Style

The brand's visual identity is **clean, minimal, and modern**. Design materials use generous white/negative space, simple typography, and the brand's signature colors. The aesthetic leans toward the kind of design sensibility you'd see from high-end streetwear brands or contemporary design studios — confident, understated, and intentional. Every visual choice communicates that this is a brand that understands design, which is critical because the product IS design.

## 2.4 Typography

The brand uses clean, modern typefaces. Text is typically set in lowercase to match the brand name styling. The typographic choices reinforce the minimal, contemporary feel. In content posts, the typography is part of the humor and identity — for example, one planned post explores how the same sentence feels completely different in Comic Sans vs Helvetica vs the brand's own font.

## 2.5 Design Tools & Constraints

All design work is done in **Photopea** (browser-based, free) and **DaVinci Resolve** (free video editor). There is no budget for Adobe Creative Suite, paid plugins, or professional production equipment. This constraint is non-negotiable and all content ideas must be executable within these tools. This means:

- No complex 3D renders
- No After Effects-level motion graphics
- No professional video shoots
- No paid templates or assets
- Everything must be achievable by one person with free software

This constraint was repeatedly emphasized throughout our discussions and is a hard filter for every content idea. If an idea requires tools or resources beyond Photopea and DaVinci Resolve, it is rejected regardless of how good the concept is.

---

# SECTION 3: TARGET AUDIENCE

## 3.1 Primary Demographic

The target audience is **Gen Z** (approximately ages 16-26), with a particular focus on the segment that:

- Cares about their phone's aesthetic setup (wallpaper, widgets, app layout, icon packs)
- Engages with meme culture and internet humor
- Has short attention spans and scrolls quickly
- Finds things funny for irrational reasons
- Communicates in a specific internet language (brainrot, "ts was NOT the wind 🥀", "let him cook", "nah he tweaking 💀", etc.)
- Values authenticity over polish
- Can detect when a brand is trying too hard
- Shares content in group chats with no caption as a form of communication

## 3.2 Audience Behavior Patterns

This audience:

- **Does not respond to traditional marketing language.** Words like "premium," "exclusive," "elevate your experience" are invisible to them or actively repel them.
- **Scrolls fast.** Content has 1-2 seconds to stop the scroll or it's gone forever.
- **Engages with chaos.** Content that is too polished, too explained, or too logical feels corporate and gets ignored. Content that has an element of irrationality, absurdity, or unexpectedness gets saved, shared, and commented on.
- **Shares content as communication.** They don't just like posts — they send them to friends as a way of saying something without saying it. A post that gets sent to a group chat with no caption is the highest form of engagement.
- **Values specificity over relatability.** A broad observation like "mondays are hard" gets a nose exhale. A hyper-specific scenario like "the urge to reorganize your entire room peaks when you have an assignment due in 3 hours" gets a screenshot and a tag.
- **Finds humor in format and delivery, not just content.** The same joke can be unfunny as text and hilarious as a fake legal document or a fake app store review. The FORMAT is often the joke.
- **Has a specific comment language.** When content hits right, the comments look like: "ts was NOT the wind 🥀", "nah bro thought they was slick w it", "run it back", "what was bro cooking 💀", reaction gifs, and tags with no text. This is the type of engagement we are designing for.

## 3.3 What This Audience Does NOT Want

- Overproduced brand content that looks like an ad
- Motivational quotes or hustle culture energy
- Brands that explain their own jokes
- Content that feels like it was made by a marketing team
- Posts that try to be relatable but use slightly outdated internet language
- Wallpaper reveals with dramatic buildups that don't deliver
- Tutorial-style content that looks like a middle-aged man explaining software

## 3.4 Secondary Audience

There is a secondary audience of **design-conscious young adults** (early-to-mid 20s) who may not engage with brainrot meme culture but appreciate clean design, minimal aesthetics, and well-crafted digital products. These people are reached through the design-flex posts (redesigning ugly things, sped-up design processes, color theory content) rather than the humor posts. However, the primary content strategy is built around the Gen Z meme-native audience because they drive the sharing and virality that a zero-budget brand needs for growth.

---

# SECTION 4: PRODUCT

## 4.1 What Waupho Sells

Phone wallpapers. Digital images designed to be used as lock screen and home screen backgrounds on smartphones. Sold through waupho.com.

## 4.2 Price Point

Approximately $2 per wallpaper or wallpaper pack. This price point is intentional — low enough to be an impulse purchase, especially for the target demographic (many of whom are students or young people without significant disposable income). The low price is also used as content itself (one planned reel shows a 6-hour design process sped up with the text "6 hours. $2." at the end — the time-to-price gap is the joke and simultaneously communicates the value proposition).

## 4.3 Product Presentation Philosophy

This is one of the most critical strategic decisions discussed at length throughout our conversations. The core principle is:

**Wallpapers are almost never the centerpiece of content.**

The reason: if you build up hype around a wallpaper reveal and the audience doesn't think the wallpaper lives up to the hype, the entire post backfires. The brand gets subconsciously clowned on instead of celebrated. This is a real and significant risk because aesthetic judgment is subjective — what looks amazing to the designer might look mid to a viewer, and on social media, that split-second judgment determines whether the brand gains credibility or loses it.

The solution is a framework we developed:

### The Wallpaper Presentation Framework

1. **Hype = shitpost wallpaper. No hype = real wallpaper. Never hype a real wallpaper.** If there's dramatic music, explosions, "let him cook" energy, the reveal should be something deliberately absurd or funny — never a wallpaper you genuinely want people to buy.

2. **When wallpapers appear in content, they should be incidental, not presented.** A wallpaper visible in the background of a lockscreen notification screenshot is fine — the notification is the content, the wallpaper is just there looking good without being judged. A wallpaper being dramatically revealed as the climax of a reel is risky.

3. **When wallpapers ARE the focus, the premise must be absurd enough that quality is irrelevant.** "We made wallpapers using fast food brand colors" works because nobody expects a KFC-colored wallpaper to look good. If it looks bad, that's funny. If it looks good, that's impressive. Either outcome works. Same logic applies to "wallpapers from fridge colors," "wallpapers based on zodiac signs" (where the roasts are the content), "wallpapers inspired by exam stress" (where the concept is the content), etc.

4. **Interactive formats protect wallpapers from judgment.** "Your month = your wallpaper" works because people engage with the game mechanic (checking their month, tagging friends born in certain months) rather than critically evaluating the wallpaper quality.

5. **Absence can be funnier than presence.** "We tried to make the ugliest wallpaper and it accidentally went hard" — instead of showing the result and risking the audience disagreeing, the last slide says "we can't show you. legal reasons." No wallpaper to judge. The absence is funnier than any result could be.

6. **The shitpost flash technique.** Developed specifically for the launch reel (Post 1b). When a reel involves setting a wallpaper, a split-second flash of a completely unhinged/absurd wallpaper appears before the real wallpaper. This redirects the audience's attention and judgment toward the absurd flash image, protecting the real wallpaper from harsh scrutiny. Even if the real wallpaper doesn't blow people away, the shitpost flash is what they'll comment about, rewatch for, and share.

## 4.4 Product Themes

One specific wallpaper theme mentioned is a **cassette tape theme** — wallpapers designed around the visual aesthetic of cassette tapes. This theme is used in the "your month = your wallpaper" post where each month corresponds to a different cassette-themed wallpaper. Other themes exist but the constraint established is that **no content idea should require creating a new wallpaper theme specifically for the post to work.** Content must work with existing product or must not depend on showing wallpapers at all.

---

# SECTION 5: CONTENT STRATEGY

## 5.1 Platform

**Instagram** is the primary platform. Content is designed for Instagram's formats: static posts, carousels (multi-slide posts), and reels (short-form video). The visual-first nature of Instagram aligns with a visual product (wallpapers), and the target demographic is highly active on the platform.

## 5.2 Content Pillars

The content falls into several categories, though these are not rigid — many posts blend categories:

### Pillar 1: Humor/Meme Posts (Primary)
The majority of content. These are posts designed to be funny, shareable, and engaging without directly selling anything. They build the brand's personality, attract followers, and create the kind of engagement (shares, saves, comments, tags) that drives organic growth. Examples: fake app reviews, fake legal documents, fake shipping confirmations, absurd tier lists, hyper-specific observations.

### Pillar 2: Brand Personality Posts
Posts that establish who waupho is as a brand — self-aware, self-deprecating, honest about being small and broke, but confident in the design work. Examples: the org chart where every position is waupho, the "headquarters" photo of a messy desk, the fake employee of the month certificate, the google search "how to make people buy wallpapers."

### Pillar 3: Design Flex Posts
Posts that demonstrate design skill without directly showcasing wallpapers. These build credibility and attract the design-conscious secondary audience. Examples: redesigning ugly university registration pages, sped-up design processes, the font comparison carousel, the color ranking carousel.

### Pillar 4: Wallpaper-Adjacent Posts
Posts that involve wallpapers but protect them using the framework described in Section 4.3. Examples: fast food brand color wallpapers, zodiac wallpapers, fridge color wallpapers, the iOS settings page wallpaper, the exam stress wallpapers.

### Pillar 5: Raw/Authentic Posts
Undesigned or minimally designed posts that feel like they came from a personal account, not a brand. Examples: the 4,100 notifications screenshot, the camera roll scroll reel, the desk photo, the screen time screenshot. These break up the designed content and make the account feel human.

## 5.3 Content Formats

### Static Posts
Single-image posts. Used for punchy one-liners, fake documents (warranty cards, certificates, legal notices, shipping confirmations), pie charts, venn diagrams, flowcharts, and screenshots. Quick to make in Photopea. These are the lowest-effort format.

### Carousels
Multi-slide posts (typically 5-8 slides, up to 13 for the zodiac/month posts). Used for lists, escalating jokes, fake reviews, tier lists, guides, and any content that benefits from a sequential reveal. The first slide (thumbnail) must stop the scroll — it's the most important slide. Carousels have high save rates on Instagram because people bookmark them to read later or share later.

### Reels
Short-form video, typically 6-15 seconds. Used sparingly because they require more effort (screen recording + editing in DaVinci Resolve). The reels planned are deliberately low-effort in production:
- Screen recordings (camera roll scrolling, notes app scrolling, spotify queue, typing and deleting captions, alarm lists, google searches, timer setting)
- Sped-up design processes
- The Post 1b shitpost wallpaper reel (the most produced reel, but still achievable with screen recording + basic DaVinci editing)

No reels require filming with a camera, showing a face, professional lighting, or complex editing. Every reel is a screen recording with optional text overlay and audio added in DaVinci.

## 5.4 Content Tone & Voice

The brand voice is:

- **Lowercase energy.** Everything feels casual, unhurried, unbothered. The brand doesn't shout.
- **Self-aware.** The brand knows it's one person selling $2 wallpapers. It doesn't pretend to be bigger than it is. This honesty is a strength, not a weakness.
- **Dry humor.** The funniest posts are delivered completely deadpan. No laughing emojis in captions. No "😂😂😂 WHO CAN RELATE." The brand says something absurd and moves on as if it's normal.
- **Never explains the joke.** If people don't get it, they don't get it. Explaining kills the humor and makes the brand look like it's trying too hard.
- **Speaks the audience's language without trying to be the audience.** The brand understands Gen Z internet culture but doesn't mimic it in a way that feels like a corporate social media manager googled "gen z slang." The humor is native, not performed.
- **Confident but not arrogant.** The brand believes in its product and design skill but expresses this through the quality of the work, not through claims or hype.

## 5.5 What the Content is NOT

This was emphasized repeatedly:

- **Not tutorial content.** No "how to customize your phone" videos. No "top 10 wallpaper tips." This is not an educational account.
- **Not aesthetic/mood board content.** Not a Pinterest-style account that just posts pretty images. Every post has a concept, a joke, or a purpose beyond looking nice.
- **Not hustle culture content.** No "day in the life of a young entrepreneur" reels. No "grind never stops" energy. No motivational quotes.
- **Not traditional marketing.** No "NEW DROP 🔥 LINK IN BIO" posts. No sales language. No urgency tactics. No "limited time only." The brand sells by being interesting and likeable, not by selling.
- **Not overproduced.** Nothing should look like it took a team of people to make. The lo-fi, one-person energy is a feature, not a bug.

---

# SECTION 6: THE LAUNCH SEQUENCE

## 6.1 Overview

The Instagram account launches with a specific sequence of three posts designed to create intrigue without over-explaining. The launch does not announce itself as a launch. There is no "WE'RE LIVE" post. The account simply starts existing.

## 6.2 Post 1a: The Folly Carousel

The first thing anyone sees on the account. A carousel where the thumbnail/cover is a solid folly-colored square. Nothing else. No text on the cover. When swiped, the inside slides reveal minimal information: "est. 2025" / "hong kong" / "waupho.com" — each on a different brand color slide. This tells you what you need to know (when, where, website) without telling you what the brand is or what it does. The mystery is intentional. The solid color thumbnail is visually striking in a feed full of busy images — it stops the scroll through contrast.

## 6.3 Post 1b: The Shitpost Wallpaper Reel

The reel that was developed through extensive iteration throughout our conversations. This is the most strategically important piece of launch content because it's the first piece of content that actually DOES something (as opposed to 1a and 1c which are pure brand presence).

**Structure:**
- Opens mid-scroll on waupho.com. The website is already on screen. Bass boosted brainrot music is already playing. Text appears from frame one (something like "nah he tweaking 💀") so the audience immediately knows something is happening and stays.
- Thumb taps a wallpaper thumbnail on the website. Screenshots it.
- Quick cut to phone settings, slightly sped up to maintain pace. Selecting the wallpaper.
- Hits "set." Screen flashes white.
- For a split second (3-4 frames), a completely unhinged shitpost wallpaper appears. Something so absurd and unexpected that people need to rewind — like a photo of a raw chicken, a mugshot, something that makes zero sense as a wallpaper.
- Immediately after, the actual wallpaper is properly installed on the lock screen. Explosion gifs on all four corners. Music drops harder.
- Hard cut to black. Logo. End.
- Total duration: approximately 10 seconds.

**Why this works (the strategic reasoning):**
- The shitpost flash redirects attention away from judging the real wallpaper
- Even if the real wallpaper doesn't blow someone away, the flash is what they'll talk about
- Creates rewatch value (people go back to see what the flash was)
- Generates the exact type of comments we want ("ts was NOT the wind 🥀", "run it back bro", "what was that 💀", "nah he thought he was slick w it")
- Simultaneously shows the website (waupho.com), demonstrates how to get a wallpaper (screenshot from site → set as wallpaper), and entertains — all without feeling like a tutorial or an ad
- The brainrot editing style speaks the audience's visual language

**How this idea was developed:**
This reel went through multiple iterations during our conversations:
1. Originally conceived as a cinematic bedroom reel with someone walking to their phone — rejected because it required filming equipment, a presentable room, and was too produced for the brand's constraints
2. Changed to a plain screen recording of changing a wallpaper — rejected because it was boring, had no hook, no story, and looked like a tutorial from a middle-aged man
3. Changed to a quick "nah 💀" before/after — rejected because it was too abrupt, killed any funny potential, had no buildup or storytelling
4. Changed to a brainrot-style website screen recording — better energy but still had the wallpaper reveal risk
5. Final version: the shitpost flash was added as the solution that simultaneously solves the reveal risk, adds rewatch value, generates the right comments, and creates a story arc within 10 seconds

**The first segment problem and solution:**
A key challenge identified was that the first segment (browsing the website, screenshotting, going to settings) is inherently boring and could cause people to scroll away before reaching the payoff. The solution combines three techniques:
1. Start mid-action (website already open, no typing URL or opening Safari)
2. Text on screen from frame one that creates expectation ("nah he tweaking 💀" implies something is about to happen)
3. Slightly speed up the settings/selection process to signal "payoff incoming"

This gets through the first segment in 4-5 seconds, which is short enough for short attention spans to hold.

## 6.4 Post 1c: The Cyan Carousel

A solid cyan-colored carousel. Thumbnail is just cyan. Inside: "you're still here?" This mirrors the structure of 1a but with the secondary brand color and a different message. The "you're still here?" acknowledges that someone has now looked at three posts from a brand they know nothing about. It's self-aware and slightly cheeky. It rewards the curious viewer with a moment of humor.

## 6.5 Launch Sequence Logic

The three posts together create a profile grid that is visually striking: folly square, a reel thumbnail, cyan square. When someone lands on the profile, they see bold colors, minimal information, and one piece of actual content. The brand doesn't over-explain itself. It trusts the audience to be curious enough to explore. The website URL is in the bio and mentioned in post 1a, so anyone who wants to know more can find out, but the brand never begs for attention or over-sells.

---

# SECTION 7: THE CONTENT IDEAS (FULL LIST)

## 7.1 Content Idea Development Process

The content ideas went through multiple rounds of development and critique. Key issues identified and addressed:

### Problem 1: Twitter-humor-turned-into-slides
Early ideas were essentially relatable observations formatted as Instagram carousels. "Things that exist for no reason," "lies we all believe," etc. These are fine but not original — hundreds of accounts post this format. The fix was to ensure every carousel has something unexpected, irrational, or format-breaking that elevates it beyond generic relatable content.

### Problem 2: Wallpaper reveal risk
Many early ideas centered on revealing wallpapers as the climax of a post or reel. This was identified as a significant risk (see Section 4.3). The fix was the Wallpaper Presentation Framework.

### Problem 3: Same gimmick applied to every post
In one iteration, the solution to "make posts more interesting" was to add "one unexpected slide" to every carousel. This was identified as formulaic — if every post has the same trick, the trick becomes predictable and loses its power. The fix was to ensure variety in HOW the unexpected element manifests (sometimes it's a format break, sometimes it's an escalation, sometimes it's an absence, sometimes there is no twist at all because not every post needs one).

### Problem 4: Ideas requiring resources beyond constraints
Some ideas required filming video, having a presentable space, using software beyond Photopea/DaVinci, or creating new wallpaper themes specifically for the post. These were filtered out. Every surviving idea is executable by one person with free software.

### Problem 5: Content that doesn't stop the scroll
Every idea was evaluated against the question "would someone scrolling quickly actually stop for this?" If the answer was no — if the thumbnail or first frame didn't create immediate curiosity or visual interest — the idea was reworked or cut.

## 7.2 The Complete Post List

### Posts 1a, 1b, 1c
Launch sequence as described in Section 6.

### Post 2: The Notifications Screenshot
A screenshot of the phone's notification folder showing 4,100 unread notifications. Caption: "later." This is a raw/authentic post — no design, just a screenshot. It establishes the brand's casual, unbothered personality. The absurd number of notifications is relatable to the audience and the one-word caption reinforces the brand voice.

### Post 3: Reviews No One Asked For
Carousel of fake App Store reviews for default phone apps. The key to making this land is that each review is written with disproportionate emotional investment — someone having a genuine emotional crisis over a calculator app. Google Maps 3 stars: "got me there. didn't get me back. i live here now." Calculator 5 stars: "showed me exactly how broke i am. respect for not sugarcoating it." Clock 1 star: "i didn't ask to be perceived like this every single hour." 6 slides.

### Post 4: The Mom Notification
Static: lockscreen screenshot with a waupho wallpaper visible, notification from "Mom" saying "are you ever going to get a real job." The wallpaper is incidental — the notification is the content. People share this for the notification, the wallpaper just happens to be there looking good in the background. This is the wallpaper-as-background-not-centerpiece principle in action.

### Post 5: The Google Search
Static: screenshot of googling "how to make people buy wallpapers" with increasingly unhelpful autocomplete suggestions. Looks raw and unedited. The honesty of a brand posting their actual search about how to sell their product is the humor. It doesn't feel like content strategy — it feels like someone forgot this was a business account.

### Post 6: The Org Chart
Static: corporate org chart where every position (CEO, CFO, Head of Design, Marketing, Intern) is waupho. Clean corporate design. One position buried in the middle is something that shouldn't be on an org chart — like "Emotional Support" — and it's also waupho.

### Post 7: Emails We Almost Sent
Carousel of escalating professor emails. Each slide looks like an actual email draft screenshot rather than a designed content slide. The lack of design on a design brand's page is unexpected. The emails escalate from professional to unhinged. The last slide is an email addressed to the wrong person entirely — sent to "mom" instead of the professor.

### Post 8: Headquarters
Static: an actual unedited photo of the workspace/desk. Messy, real, unstaged. Caption: "headquarters." The gap between the corporate word and the reality of the photo is the joke. A real photo on a brand page stops the scroll because it looks out of place among designed content.

### Post 9: The Apartment Listing
Carousel: a fake real estate listing for your phone's home screen. "Spacious layout. Recently renovated (new wallpaper). Minimalist aesthetic. Good lighting. 3 apps included. No clutter. Accepting applications." Each slide mimics actual property listing format — photos of the "property" (phone screenshots), floor plan (app layout diagram), amenities list. The wallpaper appears naturally as part of the "property" without being presented or hyped. 5-6 slides.

### Post 10: Camera Roll Reel
Easy reel: screen recording scrolling through camera roll. 90% design files. One photo buried in the middle that genuinely raises questions — something weird, unexplained. Comments become people asking about that photo. The brand never answers. The mystery is the engagement driver.

### Post 11: Out of Stock
Static: fake e-commerce "out of stock" page for something intangible. "Creativity — currently out of stock. Expected restock: unclear." Designed to look like an actual product page with the brand's colors.

### Post 12: Fast Food Brand Wallpapers
Carousel: wallpapers made using color palettes extracted from fast food brand logos. McDonald's, KFC, Subway. Brand → extracted colors → wallpaper. One entry is not a fast food brand at all — a funeral home or the IRS — presented with identical energy and zero acknowledgment that it's out of place. The absurd premise means wallpaper quality is irrelevant.

### Post 13: Storage Breakdown
Static: phone storage screenshot (real or realistic). "Photos: 2GB. Apps: 4GB. Wallpapers nobody asked for: 47GB." Must look like an actual settings screenshot to land properly.

### Post 14: Things That Don't Exist But Should
Carousel of inventions for problems everyone has but nobody talks about. "A font that automatically makes your email sound less passive aggressive." "An app that tells you if the person who said 'let's hang out soon' actually means it." "A notification that says 'they're typing but they're going to delete it so don't get excited.'" The specificity of each invention is what makes it funny. 5-6 slides.

### Post 15: Design Process Reel
Easy reel: sped-up screen recording of designing in Photopea at 4x speed. At some point something clearly goes wrong — wrong layer selected, colors go insane, undo gets spammed. The mistake is left in. Text at end: "6 hours. $2." The wallpaper is barely visible at speed so it can't be judged. The time-to-price ratio is the joke.

### Post 16: Terms of Service for Friendships
Static: fake terms of service document. "By being friends with us you agree to: respond to memes within 4 hours. never screenshot without permission. pretend to laugh at jokes that didn't land. never bring up that one thing from 2019." Dense legal text, small font, designed like an actual legal document.

### Post 17: Your Month = Your Wallpaper
Carousel: 12 cassette-themed wallpapers, one per month. April is deliberately just a solid color with no effort — people born in April riot in the comments, which drives engagement. The interactive format (checking your month) is the engagement driver, not wallpaper quality. 13 slides.

### Post 18: While I'm At It
Static: "the most dangerous sentence in any language is 'while i'm at it.'" Clean, one line. Not every post needs chaos. Some posts are just sharp.

### Post 19: Redesigning Ugly Things
Carousel: taking things that look terrible (university registration pages, parking tickets, doctor appointment texts) and redesigning them in the brand's clean style. Your redesign next to the original. But one "original" is something that actually looks fine — like the Apple website — and your "redesign" is objectively worse. Presented with identical confidence. Design flex post, zero wallpapers shown. 5-6 slides.

### Post 20: Loading Screen
Static: fake system loading screen. "Loading social skills... 12%." Progress bar stuck. Monospace font, dark background, terminal aesthetic.

### Post 21: Brand History
Carousel: "a complete history of our brand." Slide 1: "2024: had an idea." Slide 2: "2025: still had the idea." Slide 3: "2025 (later): made a website." Slide 4: "2025 (even later): you're looking at it." Slide 5: blank. The timeline format applied to a brand that's barely done anything. The blank last slide implies an empty future. 5 slides.

### Post 22: Breaking News
Static: BREAKING NEWS lower third graphic. "local man changes phone wallpaper for the first time since 2019. friends and family in shock." Ticker at bottom with a second unrelated headline.

### Post 23: Caption Drafts Reel
Easy reel: screen recording of typing a caption, deleting, retyping, deleting. Each draft gets progressively weirder — first is normal, second is slightly off, third is a cry for help, fourth is in a different language for no reason. Finally types "." and posts. People rewatch to read each draft.

### Post 24: Fridge Color Wallpapers
Carousel: wallpapers made using only colors extracted from fridge items. Fridge photo → hex colors → wallpaper. One item in the fridge is something that shouldn't be there (a shoe, a book). Never addressed in text. People notice and comment. 4-5 slides.

### Post 25: Do Not Disturb
Static: fake iOS Do Not Disturb screen. "Notifications silenced. finally. they were saying things i wasn't ready to hear." Designed to look like the actual iOS screen.

### Post 26: iOS Settings Wallpaper
Carousel: a wallpaper that's literally just the iOS settings page, presented deadpan as a real product. Second slide shows it installed — genuinely confusing because you can't tell if the phone is on settings or the home screen. The wallpaper is deliberately useless — that's the joke. No quality risk. 3 slides.

### Post 27: Phone Face Down
Static: photo of a phone face-down on a table. Caption: "it's looking at its wallpaper without me." The personification of the phone is irrational. Raw photo, no design. Caption does all the work.

### Post 28: Zodiac Wallpapers
Carousel: wallpapers for each zodiac sign with brutally honest roasts. The roasts are the content — people engage for their sign's roast, not to judge the wallpaper. Leos get a wallpaper that's just a mirror. Gemini gets two wallpapers. Virgos get one that's deliberately 1 pixel off-center. The sign-specific trolling protects wallpapers from quality judgment. 13 slides.

### Post 29: Spotify Queue Reel
Easy reel: screen recording scrolling through spotify queue. One song completely breaks the vibe of everything around it. You skip it. Go back to it. Skip again. The indecision is the content. No text.

### Post 30: Product Recall
Static: fake product recall notice. "PRODUCT RECALL: waupho wallpaper batch #047. Reason: too hard. Users reported inability to look at anything else. If you have been affected, do not contact us. We cannot help you." Official document format. The wallpaper is never shown — no quality risk.

### Post 31: The Font Says Everything
Carousel: same sentence in different fonts. One slide uses Papyrus or Jokerman — description is just "blocked." One slide has text so small it's barely readable — "this is how you text your crush." The visual representation of texting small = being shy. 5 slides.

### Post 32: Texting Your Wallpaper
Static: fake iMessage conversation between "me" and "my wallpaper." The wallpaper is texting you. "hey." "you haven't looked at me in 3 hours." "is there someone else." "i saw you looking at other wallpapers." The possessive wallpaper is irrational.

### Post 33: Things That Should Require a License
Carousel: hyper-specific scenarios. "Choosing the restaurant for a group of more than 4 people." "Sending a paragraph text to someone who responded with 'lol.'" "Playing music out loud on public transport." "Using the word 'synergy' in any context." Specificity over broad relatability. 5-6 slides.

### Post 34: Grocery List Reel
Easy reel: screen recording of notes app. Note titled "groceries." List starts normal (milk, eggs, bread) and gradually becomes existential (meaning, purpose, a reason to close photopea). The transition from mundane to existential within a grocery list format.

### Post 35: Warranty Card
Static: fake warranty card for a wallpaper. "Covered for: looking good (lifetime). Compliments from strangers (not guaranteed). Emotional stability (not included). Making your ex jealous (results may vary)." Designed like actual warranty documentation.

### Post 36: Ugliest Wallpaper Attempt
Carousel: "we tried to make the ugliest wallpaper possible." Shows the ugly ingredients (neon, clashing patterns). Last slide: "we can't show you. legal reasons." No wallpaper to judge. The absence is funnier than any result. 4 slides.

### Post 37: Pie Chart
Static: "how we spend our time." 2% actual work. 3% pretending to work. 95% thinking about working. But the percentages add up to 107%. Never addressed.

### Post 38: Passenger Rules
Carousel: unwritten rules of being a car passenger. You are the aux. You do not fall asleep on short trips. You do not touch the AC. One rule is disproportionately intense: "if you miss the exit because of your navigation, the friendship is under review. this is not a joke." 5-6 slides.

### Post 39: Spot the Difference
Static: two identical phone screens with a wallpaper. No difference. "only real ones can see it." The joke protects the wallpaper — anyone who says it looks mid is admitting they can't "see it."

### Post 40: Folly Swatch
Static: color swatch. "this color is literally called folly. we didn't name it. we just chose it."

### Post 41: Website Scroll Reel
Easy reel: screen recording scrolling waupho.com. Chill audio. A notification drops down during recording from someone texting something funny or embarrassing. You quickly swipe it away. The "accidental" notification is what people engage with.

### Post 42: Exam Stress Wallpapers
Carousel: wallpapers inspired by exam stress. Calm blue to aggressive red gradient. Tiny text pattern "i should have started earlier." Clock with no hands. One wallpaper is solid black with tiny white text: "it's too late." The concept is the content, not the wallpaper quality. 4-5 slides.

### Post 43: Employee of the Month
Static: fake certificate. Awarded to waupho by waupho for something weirdly specific: "successfully exported a PNG without crying (february 14th, 2025)." The date makes it feel real.

### Post 44: Color Ranking
Carousel: ranking colors. Beige gets a disproportionate full paragraph of hatred while every other color gets one line. The irrational intensity toward one specific color. Last slide: folly. "this one." 8 slides.

### Post 45: Wallpaper Screen Time Report
Static: fake screen time report for your wallpaper. "Your wallpaper was viewed 847 times this week. Shown to 12 strangers on the bus. Made 3 people ask 'where did you get that.' Your wallpaper is doing better than you socially." The wallpaper having its own analytics is irrational.

### Post 46: Hiding Photos Reel
Easy reel: screen recording scrolling camera roll but you keep accidentally stopping on personal life photos and quickly scrolling past as if you didn't want anyone to see. The "hiding" of personal photos while trying to show design work is relatable.

### Post 47: Things That Don't Exist But Should (Part 2)
Carousel: new inventions if part 1 performed well. "A button that unsends a text from the other person's memory." "An app that calculates how long you can procrastinate before it's actually too late." "A filter that shows you what your phone setup looks like to other people."

### Post 48: WiFi Password
Static: "our wifi password is 'buysomething.'"

### Post 49: Emotional Stability Wallpapers
Carousel: wallpapers for different levels of emotional stability. Stable: clean, minimal. Slightly unstable: design gets chaotic. Fully unhinged: genuinely disturbing. The "unhinged" wallpaper is SUPPOSED to look bad — the concept protects it. 4-5 slides.

### Post 50: Content Strategy Flowchart
Static: "make post" → "does anyone see it?" → yes/no both loop back. One arrow leads off the edge of the image to something you can't see.

### Post 51: Screen Time Comparison
Static: fake iOS screen time comparison. Phone uptime: 24 hours. Your productivity: 45 minutes.

### Post 52: Things People Say That Mean the Opposite
Carousel: "I'm not mad" (they are mad). "Do whatever you want" (do not). "It's fine" (it is not). One slide breaks the pattern: "I love this wallpaper" — "they actually love this wallpaper." The pattern break is the subtle brand insertion. 6 slides.

### Post 53: App Update Changelog
Static: fake App Store update notes. "v1.0.3 — fixed a bug where we had no money. added a feature where we still have no money. improved stability (emotional). removed a feature nobody used (sleep)."

### Post 54: What Do You Do For Work Reel
Easy reel: screen recording receiving a text "what do you do for work." Start typing. Delete. Type again. Delete. Lock the phone. The inability to explain is the content. Quiet or no music.

### Post 55: Group Project Lifecycle
Carousel: designed to look like actual iMessage screenshots, not content slides. Group chat created, everyone says start early, 3 weeks of silence, someone sends "so..." at 11pm the night before, one person does everything, everyone presents equally, they get a B+. The format looking like real texts instead of designed graphics makes it authentic. 6 slides.

### Post 56: Privacy Policy
Static: fake privacy policy. "By looking at this post you agree to: visit waupho.com at least once. not screenshot without following. tell one (1) friend. accept that your current wallpaper is mid. these terms are non-negotiable." Dense legal text.

### Post 57: Timer Reel
Easy reel: screen recording setting a timer "time until i check my phone again: 5 minutes." Start timer. Hard cut to timer going off. Immediately open Instagram. The predictable failure. 6 seconds.

### Post 58: I'll Do It Tomorrow
Carousel: Monday through Sunday, each day saying "i'll do it tomorrow" with slight variations. Saturday is a blank slide (something happened but you won't say what). Sunday: "i'll do it tomorrow." The cycle restarts. 7 slides.

### Post 59: Certificate of Procrastination
Static: fake framed certificate. "This certifies that name has successfully avoided all responsibilities for 7 consecutive hours. Witnessed by: no one, because everyone else was asleep. Date: 3:47 AM."

### Post 60: Apps Ranked by How Much They Know
Carousel: Calculator knows nothing. Maps knows where you live and "that place you drove past 6 times before going in." Notes knows your passwords and 3am thoughts. Safari knows everything. Photos has evidence. Escalation from innocent to threatening. Each slide looks like the actual app icon enlarged. 5 slides.

### Post 61: Phone Auto-Reply
Static: fake out-of-office but from your phone. "I am currently away from my phone (it's on the table 2 feet away, I am choosing not to respond). I will return to your message in 3-5 business days. For urgent matters, they will also have to wait."

### Post 62: Alarm List Reel
Easy reel: screen recording scrolling through alarms. Each labeled differently. 6:00 "wake up." 6:05 "actually wake up." 6:10 "i'm serious." 6:15 "you're going to be late." 6:20 "you're late." 6:30 "you're fired." The labels are the content. Trending audio. 8 seconds.

### Post 63: Group Chat Character Select
Carousel: five types of group chat members presented as character select screens from a game. Name, class, special ability, weakness. The one who sends 47 messages while everyone sleeps. The one who only reacts. The one who leaves you on read but responds to the group. The gaming format applied to social dynamics. 5-6 slides.

### Post 64: Ingredient List
Static: fake nutrition label. "Ingredients: sleep deprivation, caffeine, one (1) idea at 2am, 47 deleted drafts, questionable font choices, a color called folly, zero business plan." Designed like actual food packaging.

### Post 65: Google Search Spiral Reel
Easy reel: screen recording of googling. Each search gets more concerning. "how to design a wallpaper" → "how to design a wallpaper that people actually buy" → "how to make people buy things" → "is it legal to make people buy things" → "how to start over in a new country." The search bar tells a story. 10 seconds.

### Post 66: Flashlight Usage Movie Posters
Carousel: how different people use their phone flashlight, each presented as a movie poster. Normal person finding keys. Your dad weaponizing it at your eyes. That friend at a concert. You at 3am navigating to the kitchen. Genre-appropriate typography for each (horror font for 3am, action font for dad). 4-5 slides.

### Post 67: Shipping Confirmation
Static: fake shipping email. "Your order of 'motivation' has been shipped. Estimated delivery: unclear. Tracking number: not available. Carrier: unknown. If your order does not arrive, it won't."

### Post 68: Conversation Exit Strategy Guide
Carousel: methods for leaving a conversation, presented as an actual strategy guide with difficulty ratings and success rates. The irish exit. The slow fade. The redirect (send a meme). The sacrifice (offer someone else). The nuclear option ("my phone is about to die" — phone at 87%). 5-6 slides.

### Post 69: Battery Death Reel
Easy reel: screen recording of phone dying at 1% mid-sentence while typing something important or dramatic. Text never sends. Phone dies. Black screen. Logo. The abruptness. 6 seconds.

### Post 70: Missing Person Poster
Static: fake missing poster for motivation. "Last seen: January 1st. Description: briefly appeared during a new year's resolution. If found: do not approach, it scares easily." Blank photo space.

### Post 71: Notification Lifecycle
Carousel: stages of a notification presented as nature documentary frames. "Here we observe the notification in its natural habitat." Stage 1: appears, you feel important. Stage 2: it's a promotional email. Stage 3: swipe away. Stage 4: 47 more appear. Stage 5: turn off notifications. Stage 6: miss an important text. Stage 7: turn them back on. Stage 8: promotional email. David Attenborough energy. 8 slides.

### Post 72: Compatibility Test
Static: fake quiz result. "You and your current wallpaper: 23% compatible. Recommended action: visit waupho.com. This is not a suggestion."

### Post 73: Screenshot Fumble Reel
Easy reel: screen recording trying to take a screenshot but accidentally triggering Siri or the power menu. Dismiss. Try again. Wrong thing again. Third try works. The fumbling is universally relatable. 8 seconds.

### Post 74: Same Energy Pairs
Carousel: unexpected connections. A loading bar at 99% = someone typing for 5 minutes then sending "ok." A buffering video = someone saying "it's a long story" then not telling it. Low battery warning = "we need to talk." Side-by-side comparisons. 5 slides.

### Post 75: Restraining Order
Static: fake restraining order filed by your bed against your phone. "The defendant (phone) is hereby ordered to remain at least 3 feet from the plaintiff (bed) between 11pm and 7am. Violation has occurred 364 times this year." Legal document format.

# SECTION 8: KEY STRATEGIC PRINCIPLES

## 8.1 The Wallpaper Presentation Framework (Recap)

- Hype = shitpost wallpaper. No hype = real wallpaper. Never hype a real wallpaper.
- Wallpapers appear incidentally, not as centerpieces.
- Absurd premises protect wallpaper quality from judgment.
- Interactive formats drive engagement independent of wallpaper quality.
- Absence can be funnier and safer than presence.
- The shitpost flash technique redirects attention in reels.

## 8.2 The Unexpected Element Principle

Every piece of content should have something that makes the audience's brain go somewhere they didn't expect. This is NOT a formula of "add one weird slide to every carousel" — that becomes predictable. It manifests differently in different posts:

- Sometimes it's a format break (designed slides suddenly becoming a raw screenshot)
- Sometimes it's an escalation (casual tone suddenly becoming intense)
- Sometimes it's an absence (expected punchline never arriving)
- Sometimes it's an irrational detail (percentages not adding up, an item that doesn't belong)
- Sometimes there is no twist at all because the post is just clean and sharp
- The variety in HOW the unexpected element appears is what keeps the account unpredictable

## 8.3 The Transformation Principle

This principle was derived from the Post 1b development process. When an idea isn't landing:

1. Identify the specific problem (not "it's boring" but "the wallpaper reveal risks not living up to the hype")
2. Find a solution that solves multiple objectives simultaneously (the shitpost flash solves the reveal risk AND adds rewatch value AND generates the right comments AND creates a story arc)
3. The solution should feel like it was always part of the idea, not a patch

This thinking process should be applied to ALL ideas, not just reels. If a carousel concept has a weakness, the fix shouldn't be a band-aid — it should be a transformation that makes the weakness disappear by changing what the audience pays attention to.

## 8.4 The Zero-Follower Test

Every post must work at zero followers. This means:

- No posts that rely on an existing community to engage (polls that need responses, questions that need answers from fans)
- No posts that reference previous posts (callbacks, series that require context)
- No posts that assume the viewer knows what waupho is
- Every post must be independently entertaining, shareable, or interesting to someone who has never heard of the brand

## 8.5 The Group Chat Test

The ultimate measure of content success: would someone send this to a group chat with no caption? If the answer is yes, the content works. If the answer is "they might like it and save it," that's good but not great. If the answer is "they'd send it with no caption and the group chat would respond with 'LMAOOO' or 'this is literally you,'" that's the goal.

## 8.6 The Constraint-First Approach

Every idea is filtered through production constraints BEFORE creative development:

- Can it be made in Photopea? If not, can it be made in DaVinci Resolve?
- Can one person make it?
- Does it require filming with a camera? (If yes, reject unless it's a simple phone photo)
- Does it require a presentable space or setup? (If yes, reject)
- Does it require creating a new wallpaper theme? (If yes, reject — use existing product or don't show wallpapers)
- Can it be completed in a reasonable timeframe? (Carousels and statics should be quick; reels can take longer but not significantly)

## 8.7 The Anti-Tutorial Principle

Nothing should look or feel like a tutorial. Screen recordings are used frequently but they must never have tutorial energy. The difference:

- Tutorial energy: "Here's how to change your wallpaper. First, go to Settings..."
- Waupho energy: a screen recording where something happens and the viewer pieces together what's going on themselves

The audience is never talked down to, never guided step-by-step, never educated. They're entertained, surprised, or made to feel something. If they learn something in the process, that's incidental.

## 8.8 No New Wallpaper Themes for Content

A constraint established later in our discussions: content ideas must not require designing new wallpaper themes specifically for the post to work. This prevents the content calendar from becoming a production bottleneck where every post requires hours of wallpaper design before the content can even be made. Posts either use existing wallpapers (shown incidentally) or don't show wallpapers at all.

---

# SECTION 9: PRODUCTION WORKFLOW

## 9.1 Tools

- **Photopea** (browser-based, free): All static posts, carousel slides, and graphic elements
- **DaVinci Resolve** (free): All reel editing, text overlays on video, audio syncing, speed adjustments
- **Phone screen recording** (native iOS): All screen recording content for reels
- **Phone camera** (native): Occasional raw photos (desk photo, phone face-down photo)

## 9.2 Effort Levels

### Low Effort (Statics)
Single image designed in Photopea. Text, brand colors, simple layout. Examples: the WiFi password post, the pie chart, the folly swatch, the one-liner posts. Production time: 15-30 minutes.

### Medium Effort (Carousels)
Multiple slides designed in Photopea with consistent styling. Examples: the app reviews, the zodiac wallpapers, the font comparison. Production time: 1-3 hours depending on slide count.

### Medium Effort (Easy Reels)
Screen recording + minimal editing in DaVinci. Examples: camera roll scroll, alarm list, spotify queue, notes app scroll. Production time: 30-60 minutes including recording and editing.

### Higher Effort (Produced Reels)
Screen recording + text overlays + audio syncing + timing in DaVinci. Example: Post 1b (the shitpost wallpaper reel). Production time: 1-3 hours.

## 9.3 Content Mix

The content calendar should be weighted toward lower-effort formats to maintain consistent posting without burnout:

- ~50% statics (quick to produce, punchy, shareable)
- ~30% carousels (higher engagement, more time to produce)
- ~20% reels (highest reach potential on Instagram, but most time-consuming)
