Waupho idea catalogue, 90 immediately feasible concepts
Effort key
L: usually under one hour
M: roughly one to three hours
H: several hours, but achievable with present resources
Live: final reference, audio, or wording chosen near publication
---
A. Internet behavior and ordinary-life observations
1. App reviews nobody asked for
Format: Six-slide carousel  
Default apps receive reviews from people treating basic functionality as a personal relationship. Calculator exposes someone’s finances, Clock keeps making unsolicited observations, Maps completes only half the emotional journey.  
Mechanism: Disproportionate emotional investment in neutral software.  
Effort: M
2. Your notifications have formed a government
Format: Carousel styled like an election or coalition announcement  
Email, Canvas, low-battery warnings, delivery apps, and one unknown number negotiate control of the lock screen.  
Mechanism: Formal politics applied to notification clutter without expressing actual politics.  
Effort: M
3. The 4,100-notification phone
Format: Static screenshot or two-slide carousel  
Slide one shows an extreme notification count. Slide two is a formal notice stating that the phone’s owner has “chosen peace through non-participation.”  
Mechanism: Extreme evidence followed by understated diagnosis.  
Effort: L
4. Notification performance review
Format: Fake HR document  
Notifications are evaluated on usefulness, timing, emotional damage, and whether the information could have waited. Low-battery receives “good message, poor timing.”  
Mechanism: Workplace evaluation of system behavior.  
Effort: M
5. Screen-time witness statement
Format: Notes app or fake legal document  
The phone is asked to describe how the previous evening was spent. The statement begins factual and becomes increasingly protective of the user.  
Mechanism: Device as reluctant witness.  
Effort: L
6. Typing indicator documentary
Format: Screen-recording reel  
A typing indicator appears and disappears repeatedly while increasingly dramatic documentary captions describe the event. No message ever arrives.  
Mechanism: Cinematic importance assigned to a tiny digital signal.  
Effort: M
7. Search-history succession plan
Format: Carousel  
Different searches from the same evening are presented as a chronological collapse: practical question, hyper-specific problem, strange symptom, completely unrelated historical event.  
Mechanism: A story told using only search progression.  
Effort: L
8. Screenshot retirement home
Format: Carousel  
Screenshots that were once “important” explain how long they have been waiting in the gallery to be used.  
Mechanism: Forgotten digital objects discussing their unrealized purpose.  
Effort: M
9. The photo gallery archaeological dig
Format: Screen recording  
Scroll through polished design tests, mockups, and visual experiments. One disastrous food photograph appears without acknowledgment, followed by more design work.  
Mechanism: Unexplained interruption and refusal to react.  
Effort: L
10. Estimated response-time calculator
Format: Fake interface  
User enters relationship, message length, number of question marks, and whether the recipient has posted a story since receiving it. Calculator returns an unhelpful emotional forecast.  
Mechanism: Quantifying social ambiguity.  
Effort: M
11. Message severity labels
Format: Carousel resembling warning labels  
“hey” is low information but potentially dangerous. “Can I ask something?” produces maximum uncertainty. “No worries” requires contextual testing.  
Mechanism: Hazard classification of ordinary messages.  
Effort: M
12. Unread-email conservation program
Format: Fake public-service notice  
The account explains why unread emails must remain untouched to preserve their natural habitat.  
Mechanism: Environmental language used to justify avoidance.  
Effort: L
13. Digital objects found in the wrong folder
Format: Screen-recording carousel  
A grocery list inside “important documents,” a serious document inside memes, and the actual important document nowhere.  
Mechanism: Familiar file-management failure treated as curatorial practice.  
Effort: L
14. Phone storage customs checkpoint
Format: Carousel  
Files must justify why they deserve entry. A 19-second blurry video receives permanent residency while an important PDF is deported accidentally.  
Mechanism: Border bureaucracy applied to storage management.  
Effort: M
15. “Just one minute” conversion chart
Format: Infographic  
Shows what “one minute” translates to while scrolling, replying, getting ready, choosing food, and opening a university portal. Totals openly fail to reconcile.  
Mechanism: False precision around habitual dishonesty.  
Effort: L
---
B. Fake systems, institutions, documents, and corporate corruption
16. Friendship terms of service
Format: Six-slide carousel  
Includes response-time obligations, unauthorized exposure of old photos, restaurant-decision procedures, and emergency cancellation rights.  
Mechanism: Legal structure where trust normally operates informally.  
Effort: M
17. Group-chat maintenance notice
Format: Official system announcement  
The chat will be unavailable while administrators remove unresolved plans, screenshots without context, and one member who communicates exclusively through reactions.  
Mechanism: Treating social dysfunction as software maintenance.  
Effort: L
18. Product recall for a microscopic defect
Format: Three-slide announcement  
Waupho recalls something because a dash should have been a slightly different dash, or one decorative line is displaced by two pixels.  
Mechanism: Massive corporate response to a nearly invisible incident.  
Effort: M
19. Headquarters reveal with one impossible detail
Format: Styled photo, floor plan, or fake corporate page  
Everything looks defensibly real except an elevator leading nowhere, a “meme containment” room, or a department larger than the building. Never identify the error.  
Mechanism: Visual discovery rewards careful viewing.  
Effort: H
20. Internal email chain leaked
Format: Carousel  
The team debates a trivial choice with escalating seriousness. The final email reveals the post went live before consensus.  
Mechanism: Bureaucratic escalation and implied team world-building.  
Effort: M
21. Employee of the month
Format: Corporate recognition graphic  
Awarded for an absurdly minor contribution, such as remembering the password without resetting it. The citation is excessively formal.  
Mechanism: Institutional recognition disproportionate to achievement.  
Effort: L
22. The team member’s prohibited creation
Format: Hype carousel  
Slides describe testing, internal reactions, technical consequences, and a last-minute review. The final object is heavily blurred with a notice that it cannot currently be shown. Hide small contradictory evidence elsewhere.  
Mechanism: Withheld payoff, implied incident, and audience reconstruction.  
Effort: M
23. Blurred evidence, wrong thing revealed
Format: Three-slide carousel  
An image is blurred for “legal reasons.” A later slide accidentally reveals an irrelevant unblurred corner, which raises more questions than the covered object.  
Mechanism: Failed concealment becomes the real content.  
Effort: M
24. Apology for something nobody noticed
Format: Serious statement  
Waupho apologizes for a tiny event that had no public awareness. The statement refuses to identify the event clearly.  
Mechanism: Familiar apology format with no scandal to anchor it.  
Effort: L
25. Statement regarding the statement
Format: Two-slide text post  
The first statement causes confusion. The second acknowledges that the first statement was released, but clarifies nothing.  
Mechanism: Corporate language recursively avoiding information.  
Effort: L
26. Waupho public inquiry
Format: Fake government-inquiry page  
Investigates why a simple project acquired 46 versions, three final files, and no definitive final version.  
Mechanism: Serious independent review of creative file naming.  
Effort: M
27. Unauthorized design department
Format: Carousel  
A fictional department releases a design without management approval. Other fictional departments add increasingly petty annotations.  
Mechanism: Implied internal conflict without introducing fixed characters too early.  
Effort: M
28. Corporate values
Format: Polished carousel  
Values begin plausibly, then become operationally useless: “clarity,” “attention,” “knowing when to stop,” followed by visible evidence that nobody stopped.  
Mechanism: Presentation contradicts stated values.  
Effort: M
29. Incident report: caption posted as “.”
Format: Fake report  
Timeline reconstructs how multiple caption drafts, reviews, and revisions led to a single full stop.  
Mechanism: Enormous process resulting in almost nothing.  
Effort: M
30. Waupho annual report after one week
Format: Corporate data carousel  
Reports posts made, files corrupted, tasks reconsidered, tabs open, and zero useful forecasts. Charts are technically polished but strategically useless.  
Mechanism: Premature corporate maturity.  
Effort: M

C. Interfaces, fake technology, and digital products
31. Confusing iOS setting
Format: Fake settings-page screen recording  
A setting is named “Prevent me from making this worse.” Opening it reveals too many highly specific subsettings.

32. Redesigned Do Not Disturb
Format: Static or reel  
Focus status gives increasingly personal reasons for silencing notifications instead of neutral system language.

33. AI startups someone should make
Format: Carousel  
Tools solve hyper-specific ordinary problems: detect whether “we should do this again” is real, delay bad decisions until daylight, or translate vague group-project messages into actual task allocation.

34. AI startup investor deck
Format: Pitch-deck carousel  
One intentionally terrible concept receives market-size charts, a disruption claim, impossible projections, and no clear explanation of the product.

35. Autocorrect performance review
Format: Carousel  
Autocorrect must answer for years of changing correct words, preserving incorrect ones, and intervening only when damage is maximized.

36. Settings page for social situations
Format: Fake interface  
Controls include “Ask if everyone is ready to order,” “Pretend to recognize person,” and “Leave without doing another 20-minute doorstep conversation.”

37. Notification that refuses to leave
Format: Screen-recording reel  
A notification is dismissed and returns with slightly more personal wording each time.  
Mechanism: Escalation through interface persistence.

38. Battery percentage personality test
Format: Carousel  
Not horoscope-style generic personality claims. Each range describes the actual operational philosophy of someone who lives there. The 1% user receives a short technical case file.  
Mechanism: Familiar number interpreted as decision culture.

39. App permissions becoming too honest
Format: Fake installation sequence  
An app requests access not only to photos and location, but to questionable decisions, unfinished thoughts, and one conversation from 2022.

40. Software update release notes
Format: Carousel  
“Improved stability” is followed by emotionally specific human patches: reduced reopening of the same app, partially fixed pretending not to care, unresolved issue with deadlines.  
Mechanism: Human updates expressed as software maintenance.

41. Captcha for being normal
Format: Fake interface  
“Select every square containing a reasonable decision.” The grid makes selection impossible.  
Mechanism: Familiar verification format with no satisfiable answer.

42. Low-power mode for people
Format: Fake control center  
Disables unnecessary conversation, complex decisions, eye contact, and optional responsibilities. Estimated survival time rises by eight minutes.  
Mechanism: Battery-conservation logic used on social energy.

43. Terms and conditions summary
Format: Two-slide post  
Slide one promises a simple summary. Slide two says, “you agreed already. we both know you did not read it.”

44. “Undo send” outside messaging
Format: Carousel  
Situations where an undo-send button is needed in physical life, visualized as interface prompts rather than a generic list.  
Mechanism: Digital convenience transferred to irreversible reality.  
Effort: M
45. Device listening to conversation
Format: Screen-recording or fake ad sequence  
A mundane conversation is followed by an advertisement with an absurdly literal misunderstanding of what was said.  
Mechanism: Surveillance suspicion plus semantic failure.

D. Design, making, and creative value without becoming a design-tips account
46. Unnecessary redesigns
Format: Six-slide carousel  
Useful redesigns of genuinely unpleasant systems are interrupted by a redesign of something already functional that is objectively worse. Present everything with identical confidence.  
Mechanism: Design authority quietly sabotages itself.  
Effort: H
47. Design from an object found nearby
Format: Carousel or process reel  
Take the palette, proportion, texture, and accidental details from an ordinary appliance or package. Include one unexplained nearby object in the reference shot.  
Mechanism: Genuine visual transformation with a strange source.  
Effort: M
48. Color palette from a disaster
Format: Carousel  
Extract a convincing palette from an unattractive or inconvenient scene: printer error, spilled drink, broken screen effect, or unreadable university interface.  
Mechanism: Taste extracted from a source that does not deserve it.  
Effort: M
49. One design, five levels of urgency
Format: Carousel  
The same message is designed as mild notice, warning, emergency, national crisis, and a version so calm it becomes more threatening.  
Mechanism: Visual design insight embedded in escalation.  
Effort: M
50. What one pixel allegedly changed
Format: Before-and-after carousel  
Several near-identical versions are examined with ridiculous seriousness, followed by one actually meaningful transformation described casually.  
Mechanism: Reversed emphasis.  
Effort: M
51. Design brief from a voice note, without the voice note
Format: Carousel  
Display increasingly contradictory written extracts from an imaginary brief, then show the visual interpretation.  
Mechanism: Good execution emerging from unusable input.  
Effort: H
52. File-name evolution
Format: Carousel or screen recording  
`final`, `final2`, `final_REAL`, `final_REAL_use_this`, and a later file named `Untitled-47` that is somehow the actual final.  
Mechanism: Universal creative disorder documented plainly.  
Effort: L
53. Buy our mistake
Format: Fast process reel  
A design process visibly fails, recovers incorrectly, and ends with “6 hours. $2.” The error remains visible only to attentive viewers.  
Mechanism: Commercial offer framed through production damage.  
Effort: M
54. Design made entirely from default elements
Format: Process reel  
Use only default shapes, basic fonts, and an unpromising starting environment, but pursue the result seriously. Do not turn it into a tutorial.  
Mechanism: Constraint demonstrates ability without self-praise.  
Effort: H
55. We redesigned the wrong thing
Format: Carousel  
A common complaint is introduced, but the design solution addresses a tiny irrelevant feature while leaving the actual problem untouched.  
Mechanism: Skilled effort applied to deliberate problem misunderstanding.  
Effort: M
56. The rejected version was correct
Format: Carousel  
Show several revisions with the final version concealed until the end. The selected result is suspiciously close to version one.  
Mechanism: Circular creative process.  
Effort: M
57. Design ingredients label
Format: Fake product packaging  
Lists ingredients such as 31% unnecessary gradients, 12% deadline pressure, trace amounts of alignment, and a warning about excessive screenshot exposure.  
Mechanism: Material labeling of intangible visual decisions.  
Effort: M
58. Creative process speed limits
Format: Infographic carousel  
Different stages have absurd recommended speeds: concept at 3 km/h, last-minute export at 214 km/h, naming the file stationary for 20 minutes.  
Mechanism: Physical regulation of creative behavior.  
Effort: L
59. Design based on a song without naming it
Format: Carousel  
Translate rhythm, density, energy shifts, and one lyrical idea into visuals. Let comments guess the song before revealing later in stories.  
Mechanism: Interpretable creative value plus participation.  
Effort: H
60. An honest design tutorial
Format: Screen-recording reel  
Begins with a polished step-by-step structure. Several steps are simply “move it until it stops looking wrong,” followed by one genuinely useful micro-detail.  
Mechanism: Honest intuition surrounded by tutorial conventions.  
Effort: M

E. Wallpapers and phone setups as incidental artifacts
61. Phone-screen property listing
Format: Six-slide carousel  
Advertise the home screen as a property, including square footage, lighting, app tenants, amenities, recent renovation, and floor plan. Wallpaper appears as the interior.  
Mechanism: Full real-estate language applied to digital space.  
Effort: H
62. Wallpaper warranty card
Format: Carousel  
Covers color fading caused by emotional neglect, unauthorized cropping, replacement by motivational quote images, and injuries from excessive staring.  
Mechanism: Physical-product protection for a digital image.  
Effort: M
63. Wallpaper installation incident
Format: Screen-recording reel  
A normal setup begins, increasingly unnecessary effects and diagrams appear, then everything resolves into an ordinary wallpaper change.  
Mechanism: Technical overproduction of a simple action.  
Effort: H
64. Wallpaper chat
Format: Fake text conversation  
The wallpaper notices it has not been viewed, suspects another wallpaper exists, and begins presenting screen-time evidence. Keep the writing restrained.  
Mechanism: Possessive object with access to incriminating data.  
Effort: M
65. Wallpaper tenant complaint
Format: Official complaint document  
The wallpaper complains that apps obstruct the view, widgets exceed occupancy limits, and notifications repeatedly enter without permission.  
Mechanism: The property-listing world seen from the wallpaper’s side.

66. Worst wallpaper ingredients
Format: Carousel  
Show components chosen to produce the theoretical worst wallpaper. Final slide says the result cannot be shown, with a tiny blurred preview contradicting the claim.  
Mechanism: Withheld result plus partial visual evidence.

67. Wallpaper product recall
Format: Serious announcement  
Recall caused by one element being too close to a clock, an accidental readable word, or a problem specific to one obscure device state.  
Mechanism: Existing product language plus absurdly narrow failure.

68. Free wallpaper hidden inside unrelated content
Format: Carousel  
A cultural or observational post contains a small usable wallpaper as an apparent diagram, background image, or “supporting exhibit.” Mention availability only in the caption’s final line.  
Mechanism: Discovery instead of promotion.  
Effort: M
69. Your month, your objectively suspicious wallpaper
Format: Carousel  
Month assignments appear normal until one month receives something clearly wrong, off-center, or conceptually indefensible. Never acknowledge it.  
Mechanism: Controlled ragebait inside a familiar assignment format.  
Effort: M
70. Wallpaper chosen by the nearest object
Format: Reel or carousel  
The color and structure come from the nearest random object. One object appears in the source image but is never used or explained.  
Mechanism: Arbitrary design authority.

71. Phone theme inspired by exam stress
Format: Five-slide carousel  
Visuals progress from organization to denial to compression to “it’s too late.” One version is almost completely black with minimal text.  
Mechanism: Emotional escalation converted into a visual system.  
Effort: H
72. Wallpaper versus notification
Format: Static post  
Create a carefully coordinated setup, then allow one absurd notification to destroy the composition and become the center of the post.  
Mechanism: Product becomes an unnoticed straight man to the interruption.

73. Wallpaper user manual that becomes personal
Format: Carousel  
Instructions begin with placement and gradually address viewing frequency, replacement etiquette, and what the user is allegedly avoiding.  
Mechanism: Functional guidance becomes an unsolicited assessment.

74. Wallpaper update patch notes
Format: Carousel  
Lists color, widget, and placement changes alongside fixes such as “reduced likelihood of opening phone and forgetting why.”  
Mechanism: Product updates mixed with human operating issues.

75. Wallpaper copied from memory
Format: Side-by-side carousel  
Look at one waupho wallpaper briefly, hide it, and recreate it from memory. The result should be recognizably wrong but still deliberately composed.  
Mechanism: Original product used as source for controlled self-counterfeiting.

F. Music, culture, media behavior, and meta-internet knowledge
76. Spotify emotional sabotage
Format: Screen-recording reel  
A coherent queue is interrupted by one track that destroys the atmosphere. Skip it, return, hesitate, skip again, then allow it to play. No explanatory copy.  
Mechanism: The decision loop is the narrative.  
Effort: L
77. Advertisement during an emotional moment
Format: Reel or fake player interface  
Build a sincere or cinematic atmosphere, then interrupt it at the exact peak with an aggressively unsuitable fictional advertisement.  
Mechanism: Platform monetization destroys emotional continuity.  
Effort: M
78. Songs assigned administrative roles
Format: Carousel  
Tracks are categorized as finance department, night shift, unauthorized intern, building security, and person who has access to files for unclear reasons.  
Mechanism: Musical qualities translated into office dynamics.  
Effort: M
79. Playlist terms and conditions
Format: Carousel  
Rules for adding songs, skipping someone else’s choice, repeating a track, and introducing a twelve-minute song during a short journey.  
Mechanism: Informal aux behavior formalized.  
Effort: M
80. Trend autopsy
Format: Carousel  
Document the life of an imaginary or safely expired trend: discovery, peak, brand adoption, overexposure, ironic return, and final use by someone’s university department.  
Mechanism: Shared understanding of how internet formats die.  
Effort: M
81. Meme customer support
Format: Fake chat  
A user reports that a meme “doesn’t hit anymore.” Support diagnoses excessive brand exposure, repeated misuse, and terminal caption expansion.  
Mechanism: Meme exhaustion treated as a technical fault.  
Effort: M
82. Internet reference difficulty settings
Format: Carousel  
The same joke is presented at beginner, normal, terminally online, lost-context, and “only three people know why this is funny” difficulty.  
Mechanism: Internet literacy visualized as game settings.  
Effort: M
83. Meme restored from backup
Format: Before-and-after carousel  
An older format is presented as a recovered file, then subtly updated for current behavior rather than simply reposted.  
Mechanism: Nostalgia with transformation.  
Effort: M/Live
84. Comment-section wildlife guide
Format: Illustrated carousel  
Documents interaction types without attacking individuals: first-comment claimant, context requester, accidental essayist, unrelated self-promoter, person repeating the caption.  
Mechanism: Familiar ecosystem observation.  
Effort: M
85. Brand discovers phrase, phrase dies
Format: Four-slide timeline  
A phrase moves from a niche group to broader use, marketing decks, a bank notification, and formal retirement. Waupho quietly appears in the autopsy stage, not as the executioner.  
Mechanism: Meta-commentary on commercialization.  
Effort: M
86. “Answer in one word,” but the interface refuses
Format: Screen-recording reel  
A simple prompt repeatedly receives increasingly long, qualified answers before producing one technically correct but useless word.  
Mechanism: Current one-word-response format undermined by overthinking. The format is active in October 2026, so the precise reference should be rechecked before publication. 【1-9161a7】

87. Tiny achievement certification
Format: Story-to-feed adaptable carousel  
Award official certificates for tiny wins, but make the approval criteria disturbingly specific. Current feeds are already rewarding ordinary micro-achievements, so waupho’s version needs institutional overkill rather than a direct “gold star” copy. 【2-b7554a】  
Mechanism: Minor action receives official recognition.  
Effort: L/Live
88. Cultural reference patch notes
Format: Carousel  
Explain how the accepted use of one non-harmful meme or phrase has changed over time: meaning adjusted, tone altered, usage permissions narrowed.  
Mechanism: Language evolution treated as software.  
Effort: M/Live
89. Creator handoff: polished giveaway corrupted by loaded reference
Format: Two-slide carousel  
Slide one looks like an unreasonably proper “answer correctly to win” campaign. Slide two invokes one exact, widely recognized, culturally loaded internet question that makes the giveaway immediately impossible and ends abruptly.  
Mechanism: Corporate sincerity collapses into shared forbidden or dangerous cultural knowledge.  
Handoff: The exact reference and final wording must be independently selected where the completion would cross a boundary.  
Effort: M/Live
90. Creator handoff: censored lyric examination
Format: Fake examination paper  
A familiar lyric or audio reference is turned into an absurdly high-mark question. The answer is culturally immediate, but the required “working” is impossible.  
Mechanism: Shared recall, academic over-formalization, and missing completion.  
Handoff: Select the exact current lyric or reference independently if its completion is harmful or otherwise unsuitable for me to provide.  
Effort: L/Live

Bonus reserve: 15 additional concepts
These create more selection room without padding the main catalogue.
91. Pie chart under investigation
A pie chart totals far beyond 100%. Instead of correcting it, later slides investigate where the additional percentage came from.
92. Spot the difference quality-assurance test
Two identical images are presented. Final slide congratulates viewers who saw the difference and gives no answer.
93. Grocery note becomes procurement plan
Milk, bread, eggs, structural stability, one manageable responsibility, and “a reason to reopen the design file.”
94. Things that should require permits
Not a generic list. Each permit has an application form, waiting period, appeals process, and one suspiciously personal rule.
95. Passenger constitution
Car-passenger behavior becomes constitutional law, with amendments added after specific incidents.
96. Email-tone infinite draft
Corporate, friendly, too friendly, threateningly concise, unnecessarily apologetic, translated for no reason, then “.”
97. This could have been a meeting
A tiny piece of information is delivered through escalating formats: message, email, call, meeting, conference, documentary.
98. Important-email management
A screen recording shows an “efficient” system that removes every application capable of delivering important information.
99. Official color ranking
Normal one-line rankings are interrupted by a full legal case against beige. Folly receives only “acceptable.”
100. Fast-food funeral-home identity
Several ordinary brand categories receive visual systems. One funeral-home version is inexplicably bright and enthusiastic but still plausible enough to be concerning.
101. Default profile-picture investigation
A report attempts to determine what happened to someone whose profile image vanished and produces increasingly unreasonable theories.
102. The wrong expected notification
A dramatic buildup suggests an important message. The notification is an aggressively ordinary university or software update.
103. Man changes wallpaper after historic delay
Presented as breaking news through headlines, timelines, and expert diagrams without requiring a visible person.
104. “We noticed you haven’t…” notification
A fictional app becomes too observant about neglected tasks, abandoned hobbies, and unopened messages.
105. Tutorial PDF easter-egg audit
Promote no product. Present selected decontextualized lines or tiny visual elements from the tutorial as evidence in an unexplained internal review.

This is not yet the final 60. It is the first evidence of where the catalogue appears strongest.
Future-production vault
These are not rejected. They are separated because the current one-phone, one-person setup would weaken them.
If another phone or camera becomes temporarily available
V1. Dying while trying to charge the phone
Cinematic physical literalization of doomscrolling dependence, ending with the wallpaper visible after power returns.
V2. Sophisticated outfit, deeply unserious wallpaper
Controlled live-action status collision. The person’s identity does not need to appear.
V3. We made a sophisticated friend use this for a day
Casual fragments showing the device appearing in ordinary situations and other people noticing it.
V4. Wallpaper has a better social life
The phone appears across multiple environments while the owner remains increasingly absent from the composition.
V5. Wrong 3:00 a.m. notification
A live shot of the phone lighting the room, followed by the aggressively ordinary notification.
V6. “Bro, I got a wallpaper for you”
Quick exchange, suspicious handoff, reveal, and immediate cut before anyone can react conventionally.
V7. Ferrari-life-collapse reel
A sequence of minor failures leads to the F1 wallpaper reveal and radio reference.
V8. Phone theme matched to outfit
The visual transition matters enough that genuine two-device filming is preferable to mockups.
V9. Man finally changes wallpaper
Treated as live breaking news, with a real hand and phone making the anticlimactic change.
V10. Wallpaper public-reaction experiment
Anonymous phone use in several normal settings, focusing on the object rather than identifiable faces.
If another person is available
V11. Admin A made this
Hands-only dispute between implied team members over a design or caption.
V12. Team member forced to use the rejected wallpaper
A one-day test documented through anonymous fragments.
V13. Human terms-and-conditions acceptance
Someone is made to scroll through an absurd agreement before receiving an ordinary object.
V14. “We asked our team” survey
Every response is represented through hands, objects, screens, or written cards, preserving identity ambiguity.
V15. Overproduced internal disciplinary hearing
Two implied team members evaluate an extremely minor posting error.
If a private location is available
V16. Fake headquarters tour
Film only surfaces, doors, signs, desks, and one impossible operational detail.
V17. Product-recall press room
A serious podium-style announcement with no visible face and an almost invisible product defect.
V18. Design containment incident
Security-camera presentation of an object or file allegedly requiring isolation.
V19. Emergency wallpaper installation
Cinematic equipment, documents, and countdown used for a normal setup.
V20. Internal meeting after posting “.”
A silent recreation of the fictional consequences of the one-character caption.
Efficient temporary-camera capture plan
If a second phone is available for just two hours, capture reusable footage before producing individual reels:
phone placed face down;
phone lighting up in darkness;
hand unlocking phone;
phone on a desk beside laptop;
phone entering and leaving a pocket;
scrolling from lock screen to home screen;
changing wallpaper;
plugging charger in;
failing to reach charger;
throwing phone onto bed;
phone beside an outfit;
close-ups from five angles;
public-space phone shots without visible identities;
intentionally awkward handheld footage;
polished slow movement;
several blank-screen shots for later compositing;
several generic reactions using hands only.
Those clips could unlock more than ten future posts.
First production recommendation
Do not begin by making the 15 most ambitious ideas. Create a test set that demonstrates range:
App reviews nobody asked for
Internal email chain leaked
Confusing iOS setting
Photo-gallery archaeological dig
Phone-screen property listing
Software update release notes
Product recall for microscopic defect
Spotify emotional sabotage
One design, five levels of urgency
Wallpaper notification interruption
Comment-section wildlife guide
The team member’s prohibited blurred creation
That first twelve-piece production set includes:
four carousels with reusable systems;
several distinctly different visual treatments;
two easy screen recordings;
cultural observation;
design competence;
implied-team world-building;
subtle product presence;
and one concealed-payoff experiment.
It is varied, but not directionless. The common intelligence is formal systems, disproportionate response, digital behavior, deliberate withholding, and controlled context corruption.

Yes. I can treat all 90 concepts as approved and turn each into a production-ready brief. Because the backlog may take time to produce, trend-dependent references and sounds should still be refreshed immediately before publication, while the underlying concepts remain fixed.
A few operating rules apply across the set:
Feed assets should normally be created at 1080 × 1350 for carousels and 1080 × 1920 for reels.
Most carousels should use 4–6 slides, unless the abruptness of a two-slide format is the joke.
Branding should range from invisible to explicit depending on the premise.
Captions should support the post, not explain the joke.
“No caption” below generally means either a full stop, one short contextual phrase, or genuinely no additional copy.
Live trends and sounds should be selected near publication rather than embedded now.
Idea 89 remains a creator handoff at the point previously discussed.
1–15: Internet behavior and ordinary-life observations
1. App reviews nobody asked for
Format: 6-slide carousel  
Branding: Invisible or incidental  
Effort: Medium
Slides
`reviews no one asked for`
Maps, 3 stars “got me there. didn’t get me back. i live here now.”
Calculator, 5 stars “showed me exactly how broke i am. respect for not sugarcoating it.”
Clock, 1 star “i didn’t ask to be perceived like this every single hour.”
Notes, 4 stars “knows too much. has remained professional so far.”
Weather, 2 stars “correct eventually.”
Visual direction: Each slide resembles an app-store review, but the profile names and review metadata contain small easter eggs.
Caption:
`more reviews pending legal review.`
---
2. Your notifications have formed a government
Format: 6-slide carousel  
Branding: Incidental  
Effort: Medium
Slides
`your notifications have formed a coalition government`
Email: claims authority because it contains “important information.”
Canvas/university app: demands emergency powers three minutes before deadlines.
Delivery app: promises food, therefore wins public support.
Low battery: controls essential infrastructure.
Coalition collapses after an unknown number texts `hey`.

Caption:
`no stable majority.`

Hypothetical 45-day posting order
This order intentionally alternates:
immediately understandable posts;
heavier designed carousels;
screen-recording reels;
world-building;
useful creative content;
cultural-literacy posts;
and wallpaper appearances.
The sequence follows the developed numbering only loosely. A literal 1–90 order would cluster fifteen similar posts at a time and make the feed feel more rigid than necessary. Important sequencing note
Although the table provides a complete hypothetical order, the backlog should remain movable. In practice:
Posts 80, 83, 86, 88, 89, and 90 should be replaced, moved, or updated according to live culture.
Posts 19, 22, 26, 27, and 30 become stronger after the audience has already experienced waupho’s implied-team voice.
Posts 61, 63, and 71 are among the largest production commitments and should not be made consecutively.
Post 68 should only be published once profile visitors have something else worth exploring.
Posts 89 and 90 should not both be used if the selected references feel too similar.
The two daily posts should normally use different formats or visual densities so one does not cannibalize the other.
The largest writing changes here were converting each idea from a loose premise into an executable hook, progression, payoff, visual direction, and caption approach. The posting sequence then separates similar mechanisms, delays heavier world-building until waupho has established a voice, and distributes wallpaper appearances so the account remains an entertainment brand rather than reading as a product catalogue.

🤖 COPILOT
Turn 16 · 2026-10-23 21:41:00
It was a good idea for internal organization, but I made the divisions too visible and too rigid in the final presentation.
The mistake was not categorizing the ideas. The mistake was allowing the categories to imply that waupho produces six separate types of content.
What was good about the divisions
The divisions helped ensure the 90 ideas were not accidentally:
60 wallpaper jokes;
20 fake corporate documents;
and 10 variations of iOS settings.
They exposed whether the catalogue had enough range across:
everyday internet behavior;
institutional and corporate parody;
fake interfaces;
design and creative ability;
music and internet culture;
product appearances;
and waupho world-building.
That is useful behind the scenes, particularly because your difficulty begins with an unlimited pool of possibilities. Categories make the blank canvas finite.
They also helped generate ideas systematically. For example:
> ordinary behavior + fake institution  
> design process + disproportionate seriousness  
> wallpaper + property language  
> current meme + meta-commentary
Without that framework, ideation can become random, repetitive, or overly dependent on whatever example came to mind most recently.
What was bad about the divisions
1. They were too topic-based
Some section titles unintentionally resembled conventional content pillars:
internet behavior;
fake institutions;
interfaces;
design;
wallpapers;
music and culture.
That could encourage us to think:
> “Today we need an interface post.”
Instead, we should think:
> “Today we need a strong idea. Which mechanism and format best express it?”
The subject should remain fluid.
2. Strong ideas naturally cross several sections
The phone-screen property listing is simultaneously:
a wallpaper post;
fake institutional content;
interface-based content;
a design showcase;
and a cultural joke.
The app-review carousel is:
internet behavior;
cultural observation;
fake consumer commentary;
and potentially an expandable recurring format.
Putting each concept into one section can falsely simplify what makes it strong.
3. It risks making the feed feel programmed
If we internally start thinking in obvious blocks, the feed could develop a detectable rotation:
relatable carousel;
fake iOS interface;
wallpaper post;
corporate document;
design process;
repeat.
That would feel less like an unpredictable cultural entity and more like a content calendar.
The audience should sense consistency in intelligence and quality, but should not easily reverse-engineer a formula.
4. Some categories were overrepresented because they generate ideas easily
Fake interfaces and fake corporate documents are naturally compatible with waupho. That made them easy to generate in volume.
But if overused, they could become a crutch. Waupho might start appearing to be:
> “the account that designs formal documents about unserious situations.”
That can be one recognizable strength, but not the entire identity.
5. The wallpaper section was too explicit
Separating 15 wallpaper-related concepts gave the category more conceptual weight than it should possess.
The wallpapers are better treated as an available ingredient across the entire system:
sometimes central;
sometimes incidental;
sometimes the payoff;
sometimes just visible;
frequently absent.
There should not psychologically be a “wallpaper day” or a wallpaper-content quota.
A better internal system
Instead of permanent content-theme divisions, I would organize the backlog using private tags across four independent dimensions.
Dimension 1: What does the post do?
A post may perform several functions:
Travel: likely to be shared or sent
Recognition: strengthens waupho’s identifiable worldview
Credibility: demonstrates cultural knowledge or creative ability
Depth: gives useful, sincere, or insightful value
World: develops the implied waupho organization
Artifact: naturally involves something waupho makes
Participation: encourages the audience to add, interpret, or continue something
These are not public categories. They are portfolio-management tags.
Dimension 2: What makes it work?
This is the creative mechanism:
disproportionate seriousness;
context corruption;
formalizing informal behavior;
literalizing digital behavior;
expectation reversal;
withheld payoff;
status collision;
escalation;
anticlimax;
false evidence;
accidental reveal;
interface realism;
audience mental completion;
highly specific observation;
polished execution of a stupid premise;
crude execution of an intelligent premise.
This dimension is more important than the topic because it describes waupho’s creative behavior.
Dimension 3: How is it delivered?
carousel;
reel;
screen recording;
static image;
fake screenshot;
process content;
cinematically produced footage;
casual footage;
useful digital object;
live-trend adaptation.
This prevents adjacent posts from looking or moving too similarly.
Dimension 4: What does it cost to produce?
quick;
standard;
extended;
equipment-dependent;
another-person-dependent;
live-reference-dependent.
This lets us maintain volume without accidentally scheduling three demanding posts together.
Example of the improved tagging
Phone-screen property listing
Functions: Travel, Credibility, Artifact
Mechanisms: Context corruption, disproportionate seriousness
Delivery: Carousel
Production: Extended
Photo-gallery archaeological dig
Functions: Travel, Recognition
Mechanisms: Unexplained interruption, anticlimax
Delivery: Screen-recording reel
Production: Quick
Prohibited team creation
Functions: World, Recognition, Participation
Mechanisms: Withheld payoff, accidental evidence
Delivery: Carousel
Production: Standard
App reviews nobody asked for
Functions: Travel, Depth, Recognition
Mechanisms: Emotional disproportion, specific observation
Delivery: Carousel
Production: Standard
This is much more useful than saying each belongs to one theme.
My assessment of the 90-idea list itself
The thematic divisions were helpful for generating the first large pool, but I would remove those divisions when creating the working production backlog.
The backlog should feel more like a shuffled deck with searchable tags:
fully developed idea;
estimated effort;
reusable design system;
live or evergreen;
product present or absent;
format;
main mechanism;
strategic value;
readiness status.
You should be able to filter for:
> “Give me a quick reel, with no wallpaper, that delivers reach, uses understatement, and requires no live trend.”
Or:
> “Give me a more ambitious carousel that demonstrates creative credibility, contains an artifact naturally, and does not use another fake interface.”
That solves the blank-screen problem without turning waupho into a collection of visible content pillars.
Final judgment
Good ideation tool, bad permanent identity structure.
I would keep the divisions in the archive because they help us see coverage and generate further concepts. But I would not:
publish in section order;
refer to them as waupho’s content pillars;
impose quotas for each section;
reject ideas that do not fit;
or let the account repeatedly cycle through them.
Waupho’s real unifier should not be a subject category. It should be:
> **A recognizable way of noticing something, transforming it, and presenting it with enough deliberacy that even the apparently stupid decisions feel intentional.**
The main adjustment is therefore not rewriting all 90 ideas. It is removing their thematic boxes and retagging them by function, mechanism, format, effort, and readiness before finalizing the posting sequence.

Turn 17 · 2026-10-23 21:42:08
Continue from where we are. Generating another full set would mostly create duplication and restart the same uncertainty we just reduced.
The 90 ideas are not perfect, but they are a strong raw inventory. The problem was mainly the presentation and classification, not that every idea was built on the wrong strategy.
What we should do instead
1. Keep the 90 developed briefs
They already contain useful:
hooks;
structures;
punchlines;
formats;
visual directions;
effort estimates;
captions;
production requirements.
Discarding them would waste solid work.
2. Remove the six thematic sections
We should stop treating the ideas as:
internet-behavior posts;
corporate posts;
interface posts;
design posts;
wallpaper posts;
culture posts.
Those were useful for ideation, but should not define waupho’s content identity.
3. Reassess each idea individually
Each concept should receive one of four judgments:
Keep: strong enough as developed
Refine: strong core, execution needs greater specificity
Merge: overlaps with another concept and should become one stronger post
Replace: too generic, repetitive, or dependent on an easy waupho formula
This is important because the existing catalogue overuses a few mechanisms:
fake corporate documentation;
software or interface language;
personified digital objects;
formalized ordinary behavior;
patch notes and performance reviews.
Those mechanisms suit waupho, but using all of them would make the account easier to predict than intended.
4. Generate replacements only for weak or repetitive ideas
Rather than creating another 90, I should probably generate 20–30 new candidates aimed at the gaps in the current collection.
Those replacements should emphasize areas currently underrepresented:
sincere posts;
visually driven posts with very little text;
valuable cultural observations;
real-world but anonymously producible content;
casual low-design posts;
strange single-image posts;
useful digital objects;
audience participation;
story-like reels;
sensory or visually satisfying content;
ideas that do not rely on fake documents or interfaces;
content where the humor comes from editing, imagery, timing, or juxtaposition rather than copy.
5. Build the final 60 from the improved pool
The final selection should be based on the experience of the whole feed:
Does the account feel varied but recognizable?
Are too many ideas using the same joke logic?
Are there enough posts with little or no text?
Are wallpapers present without dominating?
Can the production load actually be completed?
Do some posts establish capability rather than only relatability?
Is there emotional and visual range?
Are enough ideas surprising even when their topics differ?
Would someone who sees five random posts understand waupho’s general cultural intelligence?
My recommendation
We should use this workflow:
Audit the existing 90
Identify repetition, weakness, and missing creative territory
Produce approximately 25 targeted replacement candidates
Form the strongest final 60
Develop only the replacements and substantially revised concepts
Retag everything by function, mechanism, format, effort, and readiness
Rebuild the 45-day hypothetical sequence after selection
So the answer is: do not start over, but do not accept all 90 unchanged either.
The existing catalogue is a strong first draft. Starting over would likely give you another differently categorized collection with many of the same mechanisms. A selective audit will produce a stronger result and move us closer to actual production rather than keeping us indefinitely in ideation.

<br>
That is a strong way to audit the catalogue. It avoids two failure modes: defending every idea because we already developed it, or rejecting ideas simply because they are imperfect on first inspection.
I’ll use three internal roles:
The Advocate: makes the strongest case for producing the post.
The Critic: tries to stop the post, exposing repetition, execution risk, weak originality, production cost, brand effects, and sequencing problems.
The Judge: decides whether the idea deserves a place in the backlog, considering both arguments and relevant factors beyond the standalone concept.
The agents will not artificially disagree. If an idea is clearly strong, the Critic may focus on execution risks rather than inventing a false strategic objection. If an idea is weak, the Advocate must still identify its best possible version.
Because a proper debate for 90 ideas will be very long, I’ll audit them in numbered batches while maintaining one final ranked master list. Here is the first batch.
Evaluation framework
The Judge considers six factors, without turning them into a mechanical average:
Immediate content strength Is the post funny, interesting, useful, surprising, or satisfying?
Waupho fit Does it reinforce the intended cultural intelligence, intentionality, and internet fluency?
Distinctiveness Does waupho add something, or is this a familiar format with replaceable copy?
Audience behavior Is it likely to produce sharing, rewatching, saving, commenting, following, profile curiosity, or recognition?
Execution feasibility Can you produce the strong version with current tools, time, footage, and equipment?
Portfolio value Does it add needed range, or repeat mechanisms already present elsewhere?
Score interpretation
9.0–10: Core backlog concept. Produce unless execution becomes impossible.
8.0–8.9: Strong. Produce after specific refinements.
7.0–7.9: Worth keeping, but not essential.
6.0–6.9: Reserve or merge with a stronger idea.
5.0–5.9: Weak in its current form. Requires substantial reconstruction.
Below 5: Remove unless a new cultural opportunity changes it.
Batch 1: Ideas 1–15
1. App reviews nobody asked for
Advocate
The concept has immediate comprehension, broad relevance, and strong carousel pacing. Almost everyone in the desired audience uses default applications, so the post requires no niche context.
The disproportionate emotional relationship with neutral software is stronger than ordinary “relatable app” content. Reviews such as Maps getting someone somewhere but not getting the person back create miniature narratives rather than isolated punchlines.
The format also has:
high sharing potential;
easy expansion into later editions;
low asset cost;
visual familiarity;
and enough writing space for waupho’s voice.
The app-review interface provides structure without forcing the whole account into the website branding. Small reviewer names, dates, version numbers, and developer responses can also contain easter eggs.
Critic
Fake reviews are an established internet format. The broad concept is not original enough to establish waupho by itself.
Several proposed lines feel polished in a recognizable social-copy manner. If every review follows the same “app performed its literal function but caused emotional damage” construction, the viewer will understand the entire carousel after slide three.
There is also a risk of creating six independent one-liners rather than one piece that develops. The opening needs to be strong, but the final review must do more than merely provide the sixth example.
The concept can be copied easily by other accounts, so visual treatment and exact writing carry most of the authorship.
Judge
Score: 8.8/10
Decision: Produce after tightening.
This is a strong opening-period post because it is easy to enter, confidently written, and distinct enough when properly executed. It should not become a recurring series immediately. First determine whether audiences remember waupho or simply consume the jokes.
Improve it by giving the carousel an internal progression:
harmless dissatisfaction;
personal betrayal;
suspicious dependence;
final review that changes the interpretation of the reviewer.
The developer-response area can quietly create additional depth.
---
2. Notifications have formed a government
Advocate
This is visually rich and conceptually flexible. Notifications already compete for attention and authority, so converting the lock screen into an unstable coalition is a natural transformation rather than a random metaphor.
It gives waupho several things simultaneously:
culturally familiar subject matter;
institutional parody;
information-design opportunity;
subtle commentary on attention;
and a way to use phone interfaces without selling wallpapers.
The coalition collapsing because an unknown number sends “hey” is a straightforward ending that returns the elaborate political structure to a familiar personal threat.
Critic
The concept may require more reading and political-system familiarity than the payoff justifies. Terms such as coalition government, emergency powers, majority, and infrastructure could create friction for viewers from regions where the language is less familiar.
Waupho also prefers to remain broadly non-political. Although this is not political commentary, the visual language may initially suggest topical political content.
Most importantly, this uses one of the catalogue’s overrepresented mechanisms: an ordinary digital behavior presented through formal institutions. The execution needs to be visually alive enough that it does not resemble another “things as corporate departments” carousel.
Judge
Score: 7.7/10
Decision: Keep, but do not prioritize for launch week.
The idea is clever and visually promising, but it belongs after waupho has established that formal institutional presentation is one tool rather than the entire identity.
Use recognizable notification icons as the primary storytelling devices. Keep actual political terminology limited. The post should work visually before every sentence is read.
---
3. The 4,100-notification phone
Advocate
This is fast, highly relatable, extremely feasible, and ideal as connective content between heavier posts. The raw screenshot supplies the main hook without requiring extensive writing.
“Peace through complete non-participation” is a strong diagnosis because it interprets the image rather than repeating the observable joke.
The post may also attract tagging behavior naturally. People know someone whose badge counts have escaped ordinary measurement.
Critic
Extreme notification screenshots are common. `tag someone who does this` is also an obvious engagement instruction and may feel beneath waupho’s more subtle approach.
The second slide risks explaining what was already funny in the first. A viewer may laugh at the screenshot, swipe, and receive only a designed paraphrase.
The post contributes little long-term brand ownership unless the clinical assessment is much more specific or the screenshot contains an additional layer.
Judge
Score: 6.8/10
Decision: Refine or use as lightweight reserve.
Remove the direct tagging instruction. The post should be shareable because viewers recognize someone, not because waupho orders them to tag that person.
A stronger version might treat the notification count as evidence in a technical inspection, with several small details available for viewers who look longer. Still, this remains connective material rather than a defining post.

Advocate
This fits waupho’s existing website voice and design capability well. Performance reviews provide a credible visual structure, and notification behavior offers enough specific material for several good assessments.
It can be useful as subtle cultural observation. For example, a software-update notification is not merely annoying; its contribution is unclear and its timing consistently poor. That sounds like an actual managerial review while remaining culturally legible.
The format also allows easter eggs through signatures, review periods, appeal statuses, performance goals, and managerial comments.

The catalogue already contains:
autocorrect performance review;
product recall;
incident report;
public inquiry;
annual report;
patch notes;
internal email chains.
This particular idea sits directly inside the catalogue’s most crowded territory. Even strong execution may make the overall backlog less varied.
The review structure can also become predictable: familiar digital object, corporate criterion, disproportionate criticism.

The concept is good, but the portfolio does not currently need every good corporate-document idea.
Possible merger: make “Notification Department Annual Review” one section within the broader one-week annual report. Alternatively, save this for a later installment after the account’s initial content range has been proven.

5. Screen-time witness statement
Advocate
This is one of the stronger formal-document concepts because the format and subject reinforce each other. Screen-time data genuinely acts as evidence of behavior, so turning the phone into a witness feels natural.
The phone initially cooperating, then refusing to disclose what happened between midnight and 2:00 a.m., gives the object a restrained personality without relying on generic possessive-object humor.
The concept also provides two reading levels:
immediate recognition of late-night phone use;
detailed evidence, timestamps, and redactions for viewers who inspect the slides.
Critic
It still uses document parody and personified technology, both heavily represented in the catalogue.
The phone requesting legal representation is a familiar escalation and may feel like a predictable final slide. The concept needs a more distinctive ending, perhaps one that implicates the viewer rather than merely anthropomorphizing the phone.
A carousel dominated by text and fake evidence may require more attention than the joke earns.
Judge
Score: 8.0/10
Decision: Produce, with a new final beat.
The evidence structure is sufficiently linked to the subject to justify another formal-document post. The ending should avoid simply saying the device wants a lawyer.
A stronger ending could reveal that screen-time evidence was submitted by an application the user opened repeatedly but does not remember using. The exact resolution can be developed during production.
---
6. Typing-indicator documentary
Advocate
This is highly feasible and uses timing rather than dense copy. It provides much-needed format diversity.
The indicator disappearing and returning already contains suspense. Documentary language exaggerates that suspense without needing actors, voiceover, original footage, or explicit branding.
Rewatching is plausible because the reel is short, and the audience may wait to see whether a message finally arrives. Ending without a message preserves the event’s emotional truth.
Critic
Typing-indicator jokes are common. Documentary captions like “there was still hope” could sound overly produced or like an older meme format if the editing is not current.
There is also limited waupho ownership. A different account could post the same screen recording with almost no change.
The idea may perform adequately but contribute little to the account’s accumulated identity.
Judge
Score: 7.5/10
Decision: Produce as a lightweight reel, but keep execution minimal.
Do not over-narrate it. Two lines may be enough. Current audio and subtitle treatment will determine whether it feels culturally present or recycled.
Its portfolio role is useful: short, low-production, recognizable, and different from carousels. It does not need to carry the brand alone.
---
7. Search-history succession plan
Advocate
The format lets viewers reconstruct a hidden narrative, which is stronger than telling the story directly. It is inexpensive, culturally universal, and adaptable to countless scenarios.
A search progression can move from normal to alarming to completely unrelated without requiring formal explanation. The audience’s mental reconstruction supplies part of the humor.
The final unrelated search can also act as a second reversal rather than a simple escalation.
Critic
“Search history tells a story” is a well-established format. The pasta and food-poisoning example is understandable but ordinary.
The concept depends almost entirely on the exact search sequence. Without an unusually precise narrative, the post becomes generic connective content.
There is also little visual opportunity beyond a search interface unless the design introduces additional evidence.
Judge
Score: 6.9/10
Decision: Keep as a format, replace the example.
Do not produce the pasta version. Save the mechanism until a more distinctive scenario emerges, ideally one tied to current culture, a bizarre ordinary problem, or an implied event that invites multiple interpretations.
This is a reusable shell, not yet a finalized post.
---
8. Screenshot retirement home
Advocate
This has a clear perspective, broad recognition, and richer visual potential than a basic list of forgotten screenshots.
Treating screenshots as archived residents allows waupho to create:
profile cards;
acquisition dates;
intended purpose;
time since last viewed;
visitor records;
and reasons for retention.
The final screenshot of the carousel becoming a “new arrival” creates a self-referential ending and makes the format more complete.
The post can be funny, slightly sincere, and quietly observant about digital accumulation.
Critic
Personified digital objects are another recurring mechanism. If every screenshot speaks directly, the writing may become cute rather than sharp.
The retirement-home framing could also require many design details to land, making it more laborious than its likely performance warrants.
There is a risk of nostalgia or sentimentality without enough comic pressure.
Judge
Score: 8.2/10
Decision: Produce.
Keep the screenshots mostly silent. Let archive labels, dates, and institutional notes create the humor rather than writing dialogue for each image.
The post adds emotional range because it can be gently observant instead of aggressively punchline-driven. That is valuable for waupho’s broader identity.
---
9. Photo-gallery archaeological dig
Advocate
This is one of the cleanest ideas in the catalogue. It is cheap, visually natural, does not require text-heavy explanation, and demonstrates that waupho understands interruption-based humor.
The terrible food photograph works because:
everything surrounding it appears controlled;
the scroll does not pause;
no caption acknowledges it;
and viewers receive no permission to treat it as the main joke.
It also quietly reveals that real work exists behind waupho without turning into founder content or behind-the-scenes documentation.
Critic
The idea depends on the food photo being exactly wrong enough. If the image looks intentionally staged or merely mediocre, the interruption will not land.
The design tests must be visually interesting enough for the initial scroll, but not so promotional that viewers classify the reel as portfolio content.
Replay value may be limited once the interruption is noticed.
Judge
Score: 9.0/10
Decision: Core backlog post.
Produce this early. It is resource-efficient, visually different, culturally natural, and consistent with the brand’s preference for unacknowledged details.
The key is selection, not editing. The food image should feel like genuine gallery contamination rather than a deliberately produced meme asset.
---
10. Estimated response-time calculator
Advocate
This translates a highly familiar social uncertainty into a useful-looking system. The input variables can be unusually specific:
message length;
punctuation;
relationship;
whether the recipient has posted since receiving it;
whether the message contains a question;
and whether “haha” appeared before the silence.
That specificity creates recognition and invites viewers to imagine their own result.
The interface can look credible enough to produce saves and comments asking for a real version.
Critic
Fake calculators are common carousel territory. The final range, “between four minutes and the end of the relationship,” is broad but familiar.
The interface may become over-designed and slow the joke. If viewers must read several setup slides before receiving one predictable answer, retention may suffer.
This could also be mistaken for an actual interactive tool, causing disappointment when it is only a carousel.
Judge
Score: 7.8/10
Decision: Produce after strengthening the output.
The inputs are stronger than the current result. The output should use the variables in a surprisingly specific way rather than return a generic impossible range.
For example, the calculator could provide:
expected response window;
confidence level;
evidence of avoidance;
and recommended number of times to reopen the chat.
The final response can still be absurd, but should appear computationally generated from the preceding choices.
---
11. Message severity labels
Advocate
This is immediately understandable and highly shareable. Small linguistic changes genuinely alter how messages are perceived, making the content observational rather than fabricated.
The progression from “hey” to punctuation, ambiguity, and multiple question marks creates natural escalation.
The format is simple enough that visual design can remain restrained.
Critic
This territory is saturated. Countless accounts have ranked or interpreted messages such as:
“hey”
“hey.”
“can we talk?”
“no worries”
“k”
The current examples are accurate but unsurprising. Waupho risks appearing like a standard relatable carousel account.
There is minimal visual value and little reason for viewers to attribute the post specifically to waupho.
Judge
Score: 6.2/10
Decision: Replace or radically transform.
Do not make this as a straightforward severity list.
Potential transformation: create actual hazard labels applied to message screenshots, with handling instructions, response containment periods, and evidence that one label has been incorrectly assigned. Even then, the concept must move beyond familiar copy.
---
12. Unread-email conservation program
Advocate
The environmental-campaign framing is visually recognizable and provides a different institutional aesthetic from corporate reports.
“Preserving the natural habitat” of unread emails is immediately understandable. The post can mimic earnest conservation messaging while justifying obvious avoidance.
It is low effort and may work well between more demanding pieces.
Critic
The central joke is visible almost immediately and has few places to go. Every additional slide risks repeating “do not open emails” through different environmental phrases.
It again formalizes an ordinary behavior through an institution, adding to catalogue repetition.
It may earn likes but is unlikely to produce strong follows or profile curiosity.
Judge
Score: 6.3/10
Decision: Reserve, not final 60 at present.
This is competent but nonessential. It could work as a story, single image, or occasional low-effort post rather than a full carousel.
If retained, compress it to one strong campaign poster rather than five slides.
---
13. Digital objects found in the wrong folder
Advocate
This is authentic, easy to execute, and tells the joke through discovery rather than direct commentary. Screen-recording movement provides natural pacing.
The inversion between folders labeled “important” and “random” is widely recognizable, while details inside them can show waupho’s specificity.
It also requires no visible person, camera, voice, or product.
Critic
The behavior itself is ordinary and may not produce enough surprise. Viewers probably expect the “important” folder to contain unimportant material as soon as it opens.
A completely simulated file system could look staged. A real file system may expose private or irrelevant information and require careful preparation.
The Downloads-folder ending is common.
Judge
Score: 7.1/10
Decision: Keep, but add a stronger narrative anomaly.
The wrong-folder inversion is not enough. Include one file that implies an unexplained event, or reveal that the desperately searched-for document has been open in another tab throughout the reel.
This should remain casual rather than becoming a heavily produced interface piece.
---
14. Phone-storage customs checkpoint
Advocate
This gives waupho strong visual and linguistic material:
entry approvals;
residency;
attempted import;
storage declarations;
denied files;
suspiciously accepted blurry media.
The unequal treatment of genuinely important and obviously useless files mirrors actual phone behavior. It can be funny while also functioning as a coherent designed artifact.
Unlike some formal-document ideas, storage “admission” and limited capacity genuinely support the customs metaphor.
Critic
The border and customs metaphor may introduce unnecessary real-world associations for a relatively simple phone-storage joke.
It is another six-slide formalized system and may feel interchangeable with notification government, performance reviews, and storage bureaucracy.
The substantial design time may be better spent on a more visually original concept.
Judge
Score: 7.3/10
Decision: Keep in the extended pool, not an automatic selection.
The idea is defensible, but its inclusion depends on how many stronger institutional posts survive the audit. It could also be merged with a broader “phone storage operating system” concept.
---
15. “Just one minute” conversion chart
Advocate
This is one of the catalogue’s simplest value posts. It names a familiar distortion of time across scrolling, deadlines, getting ready, and waiting.
The conversion-chart format is instantly readable and can be made visually strong without extensive production.
The exam-time ending provides a clean reversal because perceived time compresses instead of expanding.
Critic
Time-perception jokes are very common. The examples currently resemble generic student-relatability content.
The concept lacks a particularly waupho-specific transformation. False numbers and charts are not sufficient by themselves.
It may produce routine engagement but little recognition or curiosity.
Judge
Score: 6.7/10
Decision: Refine substantially or use as a story.
A stronger version should construct a fake unit-conversion system with internally consistent but absurd rules, rather than simply listing how long “one minute” feels.
For example, it could convert:
one scrolling minute;
one deadline minute;
one “I’m outside” minute;
and one battery-at-1% minute
into a fictional common unit. That gives waupho more ownership.
Batch 1 ranking
Rank	Idea	Score	Current decision
1	Photo-gallery archaeological dig	9.0	Core backlog
2	App reviews nobody asked for	8.8	Produce after tightening
3	Screenshot retirement home	8.2	Produce
4	Screen-time witness statement	8.0	Produce with stronger ending
5	Response-time calculator	7.8	Produce after strengthening output
6	Notification government	7.7	Keep, post later
7	Typing-indicator documentary	7.5	Lightweight reel
8	Phone-storage customs	7.3	Extended pool
9	Wrong-folder digital objects	7.1	Keep with stronger anomaly
10	Notification performance review	7.0	Merge or hold
11	Search-history succession	6.9	Keep format, replace example
12	4,100 notifications	6.8	Lightweight reserve
13	One-minute conversion chart	6.7	Major refinement
14	Unread-email conservation	6.3	Story or reserve
15	Message severity labels	6.2	Replace or reconstruct
What this first debate already reveals
The original catalogue contains good ideas, but it overvalues ideas that can be expressed as:
> familiar behavior + formal system + clean carousel
That mechanism should remain part of waupho, but the final 60 should not contain every competent variation.
From the first 15:
5 are already strong production candidates
4 are useful after refinement
3 are better as reserves or stories
2 should be merged or held
1 probably requires replacement
The debate format is therefore doing exactly what we need. It is not only ranking isolated posts. It is exposing portfolio-level repetition, production opportunity cost, and the difference between “a decent post” and “a post worth occupying one of waupho’s first 60 feed slots.”

Why this order is stronger
Days 1–7: Establish range before lore
The first week tells viewers that waupho can produce:
writing-led humor;
screen-recording humor;
interface work;
ambitious design;
implied-team content;
wallpaper-integrated concepts;
and cultural observation.
No single mechanism is allowed to define the account too early.
Days 8–18: Deepen the worldview
The second phase introduces:
embedded value;
creative process;
internet commentary;
more elaborate fake systems;
and low-pressure product presence.
By this point, the audience should understand that wallpapers exist without interpreting every post as wallpaper marketing.
Days 19–30: Introduce continuity
The middle of the run strengthens:
fictional departments;
internal incidents;
recurring digital-system logic;
subtle product-world connections;
and references to previous creative behaviors.
The page begins feeling inhabited, but recurring lore is still not forcibly imposed.
Days 31–42: Reward familiarity
Later posts can assume more understanding of waupho’s behavior:
controlled ragebait;
self-counterfeiting;
annual reports;
product recalls;
cultural patch notes;
public inquiries;
and overproduced installation sequences.
These would feel more manufactured if posted during the first few days.
Days 43–45: Controlled escalation
The final three days contain:
the higher-risk creator-handoff formats;
an implied team reward;
the headquarters reveal;
and the deliberately contradictory corporate-values post.
This creates a sense that waupho’s world has expanded without requiring a formal narrative or finale announcement.
Operational rules for the 45-day run
Do not reveal future posts through captions. The schedule is internal. The audience should not feel a campaign structure.
Do not number recurring mechanisms immediately. Avoid “Part 1” unless the audience requests another version or the first post performs strongly.
Keep trend-sensitive posts movable. Posts 80, 83, 86, 88, 89, and 90 should shift to the day when their selected cultural reference is strongest.
Avoid identical covers on consecutive days. Even if two concepts use formal documents, one can begin with evidence, a photograph, a chart, or a visual statement rather than another centered heading.
Do not publish every product-related post as a sales post. The majority should contain no price, link instruction, or direct call to purchase.
Stories should react to the feed, not repeat it. Stories can show rejected lines, unexplained details, fictional team reactions, race commentary, casual screenshots, and additional evidence.
The weaker concepts should remain short. Not every idea deserves six slides. Ideas 3, 12, 17, 21, 24, 25, 32, 40, 43, 52, 58, 72, 76, and 87 can work precisely because they do not overstay.
Do not force visual branding onto every post. Attribution should range from invisible to explicit depending on whether branding strengthens or weakens the premise.
Review after every seven days. The hypothetical sequence should not prevent moving later posts forward when a mechanism begins performing unusually well.
Monitor mechanism fatigue, not only topic fatigue. Posts can discuss completely different subjects while still feeling repetitive if they all use a corporate document, fake setting, or personified digital object.
This revised schedule retains all 90 ideas, but it no longer treats them equally. Strong posts establish the account, weaker posts provide rhythm, product posts are dispersed, implied-team material develops gradually, and the more culturally risky or context-dependent posts appear only after waupho has earned a clearer identity.
